# Symplectic dynamics via a tube construction

**arXiv ID**: 2607.25549v1
**Authors**: Qi Feng, Jun Zhang
**Published**: 2026-07-28
**Categories**: math.SG, math.DS
**Comments**: 70 pages, 3 figures
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.25549v1

## Abstract

This is the first in a series of papers studying symplectic dynamics on Liouville domains $(W, λ)$ from a geometric approach. We employ Usher's tube construction to build a higher-dimensional Liouville domain from $(W,λ; H)$, where $H$ is a Hamiltonian function on $(W, λ)$, possibly non-autonomous. We establish dynamical stabilities with respect to a new Thompson-type pseudo-metric on Hamiltonians, bounded below by Banach--Mazur type distances. We also explore relations between the topological entropies of the Hamiltonian dynamics and its induced Reeb dynamics, examine symplectic capacities, especially the Gromov width, under the tube construction, and give a categorification of the Thompson-type metric via a generalized tube construction (called the tunnel construction). Finally, we investigate the relation between the filtered symplectic homology of the resulting tube and the Hamiltonian Floer homologies of the input Hamiltonians, as well as their iterates, on the given base $(W, λ)$.

## Symplectic dynamics via a tube constructionQi Fengfeqi@mail.ustc.edu.cnSchool of Mathematical Sciences, University of Science and Technology of China, 96

## Abstract.

This is the first in a series of papers studying symplectic dynamics on Liouville domains(W,λ)(W,\lambda)from a geometric approach. We employ the tube construction, combining[40]and[1], to build a higher-dimensional Liouville domain from(W,λ;H)(W,\lambda;H), whereHHis a Hamiltonian function on(W,λ)(W,\lambda), possibly non-autonomous. We establish dynamical stabilities with respect to a new Thompson-type pseudo-metric (inspired by[39]) on Hamiltonians, bounded below by Banach–Mazur type distances. We also explore relations between the topological entropies of the Hamiltonian dynamics and its induced Reeb dynamics, examine symplectic capacities, especially the Gromov width, under the tube construction, and give a categorification of the Thompson-type metric via a generalized tube construction (called the tunnel construction). Finally, we investigate the relation between the filtered symplectic homology of the resulting tube and the Hamiltonian Floer homologies of the input Hamiltonians, as well as their iterates, on the given base(W,λ)(W,\lambda).

## 1.Introduction

Since the introduction of a non-linear analogue of the Banach–Mazur distance between Liouville domains, proposed by Ostrover and Polterovich in 2015 to measure distances between domains from a symplectic viewpoint, metric geometry has been successfully integrated into quantitative symplectic geometry, particularly in the search for large-scale geometric phenomena and geometrically “exotic” domains. To facilitate the discussion, let us recall some basic concepts and notations. A Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is a compact exact symplectic manifold(W,λ)(W,\lambda)with boundary, whose Liouville vector fieldYλY_{\lambda}, defined byιYλ​d​λ=λ\iota_{Y_{\lambda}}d\lambda=\lambda, is outward transverse to∂W\partial W. This condition implies thatYλY_{\lambda}conformally rescalesd​λd\lambda; that is,ℒYλ​d​λ=d​λ\mathcal{L}_{Y_{\lambda}}d\lambda=d\lambda. Denote the flow ofYλY_{\lambda}byℒλt\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{t}. The rescaling of(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is then defined bya​W≔ℒλln⁡a​(W)​for​0<a≤1.aW\coloneqq\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\ln a}(W)\text{ for }0<a\leq 1.A typical example of(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is a star-shaped domain(U⊂ℂn,λstd)(U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda_{\rm std}), where the Liouville vector field is the standard radial vector field. In this case, with the notation above,a​U={a​x∣x∈U}aU=\{\sqrt{a}x\mid x\in U\}for0<a≤10<a\leq 1.

Denote𝒟2​n≔{Liouville domains of dimension​2​n}.\mathscr{D}_{2n}\coloneqq\{\text{Liouville domains of dimension }2n\}.For any(W,λW),(V,λV)∈𝒟2​n(W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V})\in\mathscr{D}_{2n}, an embeddingφ:W→V\varphi\colon W\rightarrow Vsatisfyingφ∗​λV−λW=d​f\varphi^{*}\lambda_{V}-\lambda_{W}=dffor some smooth functionf:W→ℝf\colon W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}is called aLiouville embedding(and a Liouville diffeomorphism ifφ\varphiis additionally a diffeomorphism). In particular, every Liouville embedding is a symplectic embedding, and the two notions coincide whenH1​(W;ℝ)=0H^{1}(W;\mathbb{R})=0. Throughout the paper, we use the symbol↪{\hookrightarrow}to denote the existence of a Liouville embedding. Moreover, a Liouville embeddingφ:W↪V\varphi\colon W{\hookrightarrow}Vis called astrict Liouville embeddingifφ∗​λV=λW\varphi^{*}\lambda_{V}=\lambda_{W}(and a strict Liouville diffeomorphism ifφ\varphiis furthermore a diffeomorphism). The strict Liouville diffeomorphisms on(W,λ)(W,\lambda)form a group, denoted bySymp​(W,λ){\rm Symp}(W,\lambda).

The following two definitions are the most commonly used notions for measuring the symplectic geometric distance between two given Liouville domains (see[20,38,40]): for any(W,λW),(V,λV)∈𝒟2​n(W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V})\in\mathscr{D}_{2n},dBM​((W,λW),(V,λV))≔inf{ln⁡C≥0|1C​W↪V,1C​V↪W}d_{\rm BM}((W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V}))\coloneqq\inf\left\{\ln C\geq 0\,\left|\,\frac{1}{C}W{\hookrightarrow}V,\,\,\frac{1}{C}V{\hookrightarrow}W\right\}\right.dSBM((W,λW),(V,λV))≔inf{lnC≥0|∃iW,V:1CW↪Vs.t.1C2V⊂iW,V(1CW)⊂V∃iV,W:1CV↪Ws.t.1C2W⊂iV,W(1CV)⊂W}.d_{\rm SBM}\left((W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V})\right)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\ln C\geq 0\,\left|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\exists i_{W,V}\colon\frac{1}{C}W{\hookrightarrow}V\,{\rm{s.t.}}\,\frac{1}{C^{2}}V\subset i_{W,V}\left(\frac{1}{C}W\right)\subset V\\
\exists i_{V,W}\colon\frac{1}{C}V{\hookrightarrow}W\,{\rm{s.t.}}\,\frac{1}{C^{2}}W\subset i_{V,W}\left(\frac{1}{C}V\right)\subset W\end{array}\right\}\right..\vskip 5.69054pt

Clearly,dBM≤dSBMd_{\mathrm{BM}}\leq d_{\mathrm{SBM}}, and bothdBMd_{\mathrm{BM}}anddSBMd_{\mathrm{SBM}}satisfy the triangle inequality. Note that thedBMd_{\mathrm{BM}}anddSBMd_{\mathrm{SBM}}defined above satisfydBM=12​ln⁡dcd_{\mathrm{BM}}=\frac{1}{2}\ln d_{c}anddSBM=12​ln⁡dfd_{\mathrm{SBM}}=\frac{1}{2}\ln d_{f}, wheredcd_{c}denotes the coarse symplectic Banach–Mazur distance anddfd_{f}the fine symplectic Banach–Mazur distance, both defined in Definition 1.2 of[40]. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that if there exists a Liouville diffeomorphismφ:(W,λW)≃(V,λV)\varphi\colon(W,\lambda_{W})\simeq(V,\lambda_{V}), then(1)dBM​((W,λW),(V,λV))=dSBM​((W,λW),(V,λV))=0.d_{\rm BM}((W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V}))=d_{\rm SBM}((W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V}))=0.

Therefore, in general, bothdBMd_{\mathrm{BM}}anddSBMd_{\mathrm{SBM}}are only pseudo-metrics (which may vanish on distinct inputs).

There have been various approaches to constructing examples of Liouville domains. In this paper, we focus on the following “dynamics-to-geometry” approach, which is partially motivated by the global surface of section (GSS, for brevity). Explicitly, given a time-dependent HamiltonianH:S1=ℝ/ℤ×W→ℝH\colon S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}on a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), consider the domain defined as follows:(2)WH≔{(w,z)∈W×ℂ∣z=r​e2​π​i​t​and​π​|z|2≤H​(t,w)}.W_{H}\coloneqq\{(w,z)\in W\times\mathbb{C}\mid z=re^{2\pi it}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\pi|z|^{2}\leq H(t,w)\}.

Suppose thatHHsatisfies the following conditions:(3)HHis autonomous in a neighborhood of∂W\partial W,H|∂W≡0,and​τλ,H​(t,w)≔λ​(XHt​(w))+H​(t,w)>0\displaystyle H|_{\partial W}\equiv 0,\text{ and }\tau_{\lambda,H}(t,w)\coloneqq\lambda(X_{H_{t}}(w))+H(t,w)>0

whereHt​(w):=H​(t,w)H_{t}(w):=H(t,w)andXHtX_{H_{t}}is defined byιXHt​d​λ=dw​Ht.\iota_{X_{H_{t}}}d\lambda=d_{w}H_{t}.Then it is readily verified thatλ^≔λ+π​r2​d​t∈Ω1​(W×ℂ)\hat{\lambda}\coloneqq\lambda+\pi r^{2}dt\in\Omega^{1}(W\times\mathbb{C})endowsWHW_{H}with the structure of a new Liouville domain(WH,λ^)(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}), where∂WH=({(w,z)∈Int​(W)×ℂ|π​|z|2=H​(t,w)})∪(∂W×{0}).\partial W_{H}=\left(\{(w,z)\in{\rm Int}(W)\times\mathbb{C}\,|\,\pi|z|^{2}=H(t,w)\}\right)\cup(\partial W\times\{0\}).

In particular, when the Hamiltonian is autonomous, i.e.H​(t,⋅)=H​(⋅)H(t,\cdot)=H(\cdot), the first component of the boundary∂WH\partial W_{H}above is simply the productInt​(W)×ℝ/ℤ{\rm Int}(W)\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}.
For more details, see Section 4 of[40]; following the terminology of[40], we refer to (2), for a HamiltonianHHsatisfying (3), as atube construction. In fact, our construction combines and generalizes the constructions in[40,1]. Note that the construction in[40]is restricted to autonomous Hamiltonians, while[1]focuses on time-dependent Hamiltonians defined on the unit ball111The construction in[1]uses a different normalization, so that when the Hamiltonian is taken to be zero on the unit ball, the construction yields a higher-dimensional unit ball (which is useful in[1]for treating domains close to the unit ball). In our construction (2), when the Hamiltonian vanishes identically, the construction degenerates in theℂ\mathbb{C}-component.. Here, our construction allows more general Hamiltonians on more general Liouville domains.
Moreover, in analogy with GSS (in low-dimensional situations), the Reeb dynamics on the contact boundary∂(WH,λ^)\partial(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})corresponds in a rich way to the Hamiltonian dynamics ofHHon(W,d​λ)(W,d\lambda). This correspondence will be discussed in detail in Section2.2.

Equivalently, one may viewWHW_{H}fiberwise overWW: for eachw∈Ww\in W, the fiber is the star-shaped planar domain(WH)w={r​e2​π​i​t∈ℂ∣π​r2≤H​(t,w)}⊂ℂ(W_{H})_{w}=\left\{re^{2\pi it}\in\mathbb{C}\mid\pi r^{2}\leq H(t,w)\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}.
ThusWHW_{H}is obtained by replacing each pointw∈Ww\in Wwith the corresponding fiber(WH)w(W_{H})_{w}, as illustrated in Figure1.Figure 1.The tube construction

Note that, by definition, forwwnear∂W\partial W, the fiber(WH)w(W_{H})_{w}is a standard round disk inℂ\mathbb{C}, while for more insidew∈Ww\in W, the fiber(WH)w(W_{H})_{w}is only star-shaped, not necessarily being a round disk (in particular, as shown in Figure1, the angle2​π​t2\pi tandH​(t,w)H(t,w)are related in terms of the variablett).

## Remark 1.1.

Note that the tube construction in (2) provides a special example of an open book decomposition; that is,∂WH\partial W_{H}admits the following fibration structure:(4)πOB:∂WH\(∂W×{0})→S1=ℝ/ℤ​by​(w,H​(t,w)π​e2​π​i​t)↦t.\pi_{\rm OB}:\partial W_{H}\backslash(\partial W\times\{0\})\to S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\,\,\,\,\mbox{by}\,\,\,\,\left(w,\sqrt{\frac{H(t,w)}{\pi}}e^{2\pi it}\right)\mapsto t.

Here,∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}serves as the binding. Moreover, for eacht∈S1t\in S^{1}, the fiber isπOB−1​(t)={(w,H​(t,w)π​e2​π​i​t)|w∈int​(W)}\pi^{-1}_{\rm OB}(t)=\left\{\left(w,\sqrt{\frac{H(t,w)}{\pi}}e^{2\pi it}\right)\,\bigg|\,w\in{\rm int}(W)\right\}

which is diffeomorphic toint​(W){\rm int}(W)via the mapw↦(w,H​(t,w)/π​e2​π​i​t)w\mapsto\left(w,\sqrt{H(t,w)/\pi}\,e^{2\pi it}\right). In particular, this map extends to the boundary∂W\partial Wand sends∂W\partial Wto the binding∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}. Therefore, the page of this open book decomposition is the closureπOB−1​(t)¯=:P(t)\overline{\pi_{\rm OB}^{-1}(t)}=:P(t), which is diffeomorphic toWW. Furthermore, recall that the monodromy of an open book decomposition is the return diffeomorphism obtained by parallel transport once around the baseS1=ℝ/ℤS^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}. Here, it is readily verified that the monodromyμOB:P​(0)→P​(0)\mu_{\rm OB}:P(0)\to P(0)is precisely the action of the Hamiltonian diffeomorphismφH1\varphi_{H}^{1}. Here, for the bundle overS1S^{1}in (4), one chooses the horizontal distribution as the line field spanned by the Reeb vector field (i.e., the unique horizontal vector field in the tangent bundle of the total space that projects to∂t\partial_{t}). Explicitly, under the diffeomorphism betweenint​(W){\rm int}(W)andP​(0)\(∂W×{0})P(0)\backslash(\partial W\times\{0\})above, the monodromy can be expressed asμOB​(w)=φH1​(w)\mu_{\rm OB}(w)=\varphi_{H}^{1}(w). In particular, restricted to the boundary∂W\partial W,μOB\mu_{\rm OB}acts as a reparametrization of the Reeb flow on(∂W,λ|∂W)(\partial W,\lambda|_{\partial W}).

One can modifyμOB\mu_{\rm OB}by composing with a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism onWWthat restricts to a reversed Reeb flow on∂W\partial Wso that the resulting Hamiltonian diffeomorphism on the pageP​(0)≃WP(0)\simeq Wis precisely the identity on∂W\partial W. In other words, up to isotopy of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, the monodromyμOB\mu_{\rm OB}lies in the groupHam​(W,∂W){\rm Ham}(W,\partial W)(fixing the boundary∂W\partial W). This also shows that our open book decomposition of∂WH\partial W_{H}is special in the sense that the monodromyμOB\mu_{\rm OB}is further isotopic toidW{\rm id}_{W}. In general, the monodromyμOB\mu_{\rm OB}lies in the symplectomorphism groupSymp​(W,∂W){\rm Symp}(W,\partial W), which may contain elements not realized by Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms.

Here is an example of the tube construction (2), which illustrates that symmetry in the Hamiltonian dynamics can be reflected, from a geometric perspective, in the resulting Liouville domain from the tube.

## Example 1.2.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a fixed Liouville domain andH:S1×W→ℝH:S^{1}\times W\to\mathbb{R}be a given Hamiltonian function satisfying the condition (3). For an integerp≥2p\geq 2, considerH(p)​(t,w)≔p​H​(p​t,w).H^{(p)}(t,w)\coloneqq pH(pt,w).Recall that ifHHgenerates the Hamiltonian diffeomorphismφ\varphi, thenH(p)H^{(p)}generates the Hamiltonian diffeomorphismφp\varphi^{p}. Moreover, a direct computation givesτλ,H(p)​(t,w)=p​τλ,H​(p​t,w)>0\tau_{\lambda,H^{(p)}}(t,w)=p\tau_{\lambda,H}(pt,w)>0. Therefore,H(p)H^{(p)}is also a Hamiltonian function satisfying the condition (3). Then, applying the construction (2), we obtain a Liouville domainWH(p)W_{H^{(p)}}.

Importantly, by definition we have aℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-symmetry in this Hamiltonian dynamics in the sense thatH(p)​(t+1p,w)=H(p)​(t,w).H^{(p)}\left(t+\frac{1}{p},w\right)=H^{(p)}(t,w).

This implies that the resulting tubeWH(p)W_{H^{(p)}}carries a (non-free)ℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-actionρH,p:WH(p)→WH(p)\rho_{H,p}:W_{H^{(p)}}\to W_{H^{(p)}}by rotation in the angular variable, that is,(5)ρH,p​(w,r​e2​π​i​t)≔(w,r​e2​π​i​(t+1p)).\rho_{H,p}\left(w,re^{2\pi it}\right)\coloneqq\left(w,re^{2\pi i\left(t+\frac{1}{p}\right)}\right).

Moreover, since onWH(p)W_{H^{(p)}}the Liouville 1-form isλ^≔λ+π​r2​d​t\hat{\lambda}\coloneqq\lambda+\pi r^{2}dt, this action preserves the Liouville structure in a strict way, i.e.,ρH,p∗​λ^=λ^\rho_{H,p}^{*}\hat{\lambda}=\hat{\lambda}. Therefore, the tube(WH(p),λ^)(W_{H^{(p)}},\hat{\lambda})admits aℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-symmetry via strict Liouville diffeomorphismρH,p\rho_{H,p}. The same argument works for any input Hamiltonian functionG:S1×W→ℝG:S^{1}\times W\to\mathbb{R}that admits a “pp-th root”H:S1×W→ℝH:S^{1}\times W\to\mathbb{R}in the sense thatG=H(p)G=H^{(p)}andHHalso satisfies the condition (3). In particular, observe that any autonomous Hamiltonian functionG:S1×W→ℝG:S^{1}\times W\to\mathbb{R}satisfying the condition (3) is such a function. It admits app-th root for any integerpp, so one can upgrade the rotation in (5) to be anS1S^{1}-rotation on theℂ\mathbb{C}-factor, which shows that the resulting tubeWGW_{G}admits anS1S^{1}-symmetry (again via strict Liouville diffeomorphisms).

Finally, if(W,λ)(W,\lambda)admits a symmetry itself (for instance,(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is a toric domain in(ℂn,λstd)(\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda_{\rm std})), then it is possible to choose input Hamiltonian functionsGGthat admit further symmetries, which comes from both theWW-factor and theℂ\mathbb{C}-factor. As an example, see the paragraph right below TheoremGin Section1.6.

At the level of dynamics, the Hamiltonian dynamics generated byH(p)H^{(p)}was investigated in the pioneering work of Polterovich–Shelukhin[35], where they introduced a homological criterion and applied it to quantitatively distinguish autonomous Hamiltonian systems from non-autonomous ones. Example1.2above, based on the tube construction (2), shifts the discussion to the domain level. Explicitly, an analogous problem is to distinguish a given Liouville domain from a family of Liouville domains with prescribed symmetries, and to do so in a quantitative fashion. Achieving this requires developing several new pieces of machinery. In the present paper, we initiate this program by first studying the symplectic dynamics of the tube construction, with additional Floer-homological machinery to be established in the future work[16]. Finally, we note that previous efforts toward this domain-level distinction have been made in[22,6]. Their works, however, mainly focus on toric domains, whereas the domains in our discussion admit much less symmetry. Moreover, from our perspective, rather than merely confirming whether a given domain isnotsymplectomorphic to a toric domain, we aim to derive a lower bound that quantifies this difference.

## 1.1.Thompson-type metric

Define(6)ℋ​(W,λ)≔{H:S1×W→ℝ∣Hsatisfies condition (3)}.\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)\coloneqq\{H\colon S^{1}\times W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\mid\mbox{$H$ satisfies condition (\ref{H-con})}\}.

It is easy to see thatℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)is convex and invariant underℝ>0\mathbb{R}_{>0}-rescaling. The discussion above then yields the following map, given an initial Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda):(7)U:ℋ​(W,λ)→𝒟2​n+2​by​U​(H)=(WH,λ^){\rm U}:\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)\to\mathscr{D}_{2n+2}\,\,\,\,\mbox{by}\,\,\,\,{\rm U}(H)=(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})

From a quantitative perspective, one can upgrade𝒟2​n+2\mathscr{D}_{2n+2}to a pseudo-metric space, either(𝒟2​n+2,dBM)(\mathscr{D}_{2n+2},d_{\rm BM})or(𝒟2​n+2,dSBM)(\mathscr{D}_{2n+2},d_{\rm SBM}). We now introduce the following definition, which similarly upgradesℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)to a pseudo-metric space.

## Definition 1.3.

A Thompson-type distance onℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)is defined bydT​(H,G)≔inf{ln⁡C≥0|∃φ∈Symp​(W,λ)​s.t.1C​H≤G∘φ≤C​H}d_{\rm T}(H,G)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\ln C\geq 0\,\left|\,\exists\,\varphi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)\,\,{\rm{s.t.}}\,\,\frac{1}{C}H\leq G\circ\varphi\leq CH\right\}\right.

for anyH,G∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda).

Here, the “T” in the notationdT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)refers to the Thompson metric introduced in[39]. Recall that the original definition of the Thompson metric applies to a real linear spaceXXequipped with a partial order⪯\preceq(induced from a positive cone): forx,y∈Xx,y\in X, one definesdT​(x,y)≔inf{s>0∣e−s​x⪯y⪯es​x}d_{\rm T}(x,y)\coloneqq\inf\{s>0\mid e^{-s}x\preceq y\preceq e^{s}x\}(in our setting,X=ℋ​(W,λ)X=\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)is equipped with the partial order⪯\preceqdefined byH⪯GH\preceq Gif and only ifH≤GH\leq G, ands=ln⁡Cs=\ln C). The only difference is that we incorporate the group action ofSymp​(W,λ){\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)onℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)into Definition1.3. This accounts for the fact that Hamiltonians differing only by the action ofSymp​(W,λ){\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)correspond to the same domain in the sense ofdBMd_{\rm BM}ordSBMd_{\rm SBM}, by Lemma2.5. Several metric properties ofdT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)are verified in Proposition3.1in Section3.

## Remark 1.4.

We emphasize that the nature ofdT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)in Definition1.3differs from that of another well-known metric in Hamiltonian dynamics—Hofer’s metric—wheredHofer​(H,G)=∫01max⁡(Ht−Gt)−min⁡(Ht−Gt)​d​td_{\rm Hofer}(H,G)=\int_{0}^{1}\max(H_{t}-G_{t})-\min(H_{t}-G_{t})\,dt. One possible explanation is that, by Corollary2.10, the closed orbits of the Reeb dynamics on∂(WH,λ^)\partial(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})correspond toallperiodic orbits of the Hamiltonian dynamics on(W,λ)(W,\lambda). Therefore, comparison between∂(WH,λ^)\partial(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})and∂(WG,λ^)\partial(W_{G},\hat{\lambda})should be based on the periodic orbits ofφHk\varphi_{H}^{k}andφGk\varphi_{G}^{k}for allk∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}. By contrast,dHofer​(H,G)d_{\rm Hofer}(H,G)above typically captures only the difference between the time-1 mapsφH1\varphi_{H}^{1}andφG1\varphi_{G}^{1}.

With the Thompson-type distancedT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)onℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), the map in (7) upgrades to the following:(8)U:(ℋ​(W,λ),dT)→(𝒟2​n+2,dSBMordBM)​by​U​(H)=(WH,λ^).{\rm U}:(\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda),d_{\rm T})\to(\mathscr{D}_{2n+2},\mbox{$d_{\rm SBM}$\,\,\mbox{or} \,\,$d_{\rm BM}$})\,\,\,\,\mbox{by}\,\,\,\,{\rm U}(H)=(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}).

Our first main result establishes a stability property under the mapU{\rm U}. Restricting to the subset ofℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)defined byℋ+​(W,λ)≔{H∈ℋ​(W,λ)∣λ​(XHt)≥0​on​S1×W}\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)\coloneqq\{H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)\mid\lambda(X_{H_{t}})\geq 0\text{ on }S^{1}\times W\}, we have the following inequality.

## Theorem A.

For anyH,G∈ℋ+​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda), we havedSBM​(U​(H),U​(G))≤dT​(H,G)d_{\rm SBM}({\rm U}(H),{\rm U}(G))\leq d_{\rm T}(H,G).

Obviously, the same conclusion also holds fordBMd_{\rm BM}. Moreover, there are variants of TheoremA. For instance, one can define an equivalence relation onℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)by declaringH∼GH\sim Gif and only if there exist a smooth family{Hs}s∈[0,1]\{H^{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}inℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)and a neighborhoodUUof∂W\partial Wsuch thatHs=H0​on​S1×UH^{s}=H^{0}\text{ on }S^{1}\times Ufor alls∈[0,1]s\in[0,1], withH0=HH^{0}=H,H1=GH^{1}=G, and such that the time-1 mapsφHs1\varphi_{H^{s}}^{1}are independent ofss. By the homotopy invariance property in Proposition2.4,U​(H0){\rm U}(H^{0})is Liouville diffeomorphic toU​(H1){\rm U}(H^{1}). Therefore, one can also define an equivalence relation on𝒟2​n+2\mathscr{D}_{2n+2}by declaring(W,λW)∼(V,λV)(W,\lambda_{W})\sim(V,\lambda_{V})if and only if they are Liouville diffeomorphic. Again, denote the resulting set of equivalence classes by𝒟2​n+2¯\overline{\mathscr{D}_{2n+2}}, equipped with the induceddBMd_{\rm BM}anddSBMd_{\rm SBM}. Note that in this case, by (1), the values ofdBMd_{\rm BM}anddSBMd_{\rm SBM}on equivalence classes are independent of the choice of representatives.

The discussion above yields a further update of (8):(9)U:(ℋ​(W,λ)¯,dT)→(𝒟2​n+2¯,dSBMordBM)​by​U​([H])=[(WH,λ^)]{\rm U}:\left(\overline{\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)},d_{\rm T}\right)\to\left(\overline{\mathscr{D}_{2n+2}},\mbox{$d_{\rm SBM}$\,\,\mbox{or} \,\,$d_{\rm BM}$}\right)\,\,\,\,\mbox{by}\,\,\,\,{\rm U}([H])=[(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})]

which is, in particular, well-defined. The Thompson-type metricdT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)onℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)from Definition1.3can then be restricted toℋ+​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)and descends to a pseudo-metric (still denoted by)dT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)onℋ+​(W,λ)¯\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)}:dT​([H],[G])≔inf{dT​(H′,G′)∣[H′]=[H],[G′]=[G]}d_{\rm T}([H],[G])\coloneqq\inf\{d_{\rm T}(H^{\prime},G^{\prime})\mid[H^{\prime}]=[H],\;[G^{\prime}]=[G]\}

for[H],[G]∈ℋ+​(W,λ)¯[H],[G]\in\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)}.

It is not explicitly stated in[36], but one readily verifies that thedCBMd_{\rm CBM}defined in[36]—that is, (11) above—is independent of the choice of the base contact 1-formα0\alpha_{0}(used to formulate the orbit spaceOM,ξ​(α0)O_{M,\xi}(\alpha_{0})). The following result shows that the stability in TheoremAadmits an intermediate step, passing through a contact-geometric analogue ofdSBMd_{\rm SBM}.

## Theorem B.

For anyH,G∈ℋ+​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda), we havedSBM​(U​(H),U​(G))≤dCBM​(αH,αG)≤dT​(H,G).d_{\rm SBM}({\rm U}(H),{\rm U}(G))\leq d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{G})\leq d_{\rm T}(H,G).

For both TheoremAand TheoremB, once one passes todSBMd_{\rm SBM}between Liouville domains, standard homological machinery in symplectic geometry (such as a version of symplectic homology) comes into play. Through the now-standard process of packaging filtered Floer theory as a persistence module (for more background, see Section2.4), one can bounddSBMd_{\rm SBM}from below by a combinatorial measurement—the bottleneck distance, denoted bydbotd_{\rm bot}. As a consequence, we obtain a further stability result. Denote byℬ​(U​(H))\mathcal{B}({\rm U}(H))the barcode of the filteredS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology of the Liouville domainU​(H){\rm U}(H), for anyH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda).

## Corollary 1.8.

For anyH,G∈ℋ+​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)and their corresponding classes[H],[G]∈ℋ+​(W,λ)¯[H],[G]\in\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)}, we havedbot​(ℬ​(U​(H)),ℬ​(U​(G)))≤dT​([H],[G]).d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B}({\rm U}(H)),\mathcal{B}({\rm U}(G)))\leq d_{\rm T}([H],[G]).

Here,HHandGGcan be taken to be any representatives of the classes[H][H]and[G][G], respectively.

Owing to the strong computability ofdbotd_{\rm bot}, the stability in Corollary1.8provides an efficient approach to estimating (a lower bound for) the pseudo-metricdTd_{\rm T}onℋ+​(W,λ)¯\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)}, and paves the way to the study of the large-scale geometry of the pseudo-metric space(ℋ+​(W,λ)¯,dT)(\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)},d_{\rm T}). In fact, after equippingdTd_{\rm T}with theSymp​(W,λ){\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)-action from Definition1.3and passing to the equivalence classes fromℋ+​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)toℋ+​(W,λ)¯\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)}, it is not even obvious that the induced pseudo-metric is non-trivial. Fortunately, by examining the construction of(WH,λ^)(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}), the following global quantitative relation is readily derived (see Proposition2.1): forH∈ℋ​(W2​n,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W^{2n},\lambda),(12)Vol​(WH)=1n!​Cal⁡(H)=1n!​(∫S1×WH​𝑑t∧(d​λ)n){\rm Vol}(W_{H})=\frac{1}{n!}\,\operatorname{Cal}(H)=\frac{1}{n!}\left(\int_{S^{1}\times W}Hdt\wedge(d\lambda)^{n}\right)

whereCal⁡(H)\operatorname{Cal}(H)denotes the Calabi invariant ofHH, and the third expression in (12) follows directly from its definition. Then, by Corollary2.2, we obtain the following result.

## Corollary 1.9.

For anyH,G∈ℋ+​(W2​n,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W^{2n},\lambda)and their corresponding classes[H],[G]∈ℋ+​(W,λ)¯[H],[G]\in\overline{\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)}, we have1n+1​|ln⁡Cal⁡(H)−ln⁡Cal⁡(G)|≤dBM​(U​([H]),U​([G]))≤dT​([H],[G]).\frac{1}{n+1}|\ln\operatorname{Cal}(H)-\ln\operatorname{Cal}(G)|\leq d_{\rm BM}({\rm U}([H]),{\rm U}([G]))\leq d_{\rm T}([H],[G]).

Note that Corollary1.9implies, in particular, thatdTd_{\rm T}is non-trivial onℋ+​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda). However, the Calabi invariantCal⁡(⋅)\operatorname{Cal}(\cdot)is too coarse to detect the large-scale geometric phenomena ofdTd_{\rm T}, whereasdbotd_{\rm bot}can provide a more refined lower bound fordTd_{\rm T}. The following diagram summarizes the stabilities discussed above.

## Remark 1.10.

When the given Liouville domains(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W})and(V,λV)(V,\lambda_{V})admit certainℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-symmetries (hence, admittingℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-actions generated byρW\rho_{W}andρV\rho_{V}, respectively), e.g., Example1.2, one can introduce an equivariant refinement of the symplectic Banach–Mazur distance. The key point is to consider Liouville embeddingsι:W↪V\iota:W\hookrightarrow Vsatisfyingι∘ρW=ρV∘ι\iota\circ\rho_{W}=\rho_{V}\circ\iota; such an embedding is calledℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-equivariant. Note also that rescaled domains naturally inherit theseℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-actions, since strict Liouville diffeomorphisms commute with the Liouville flow. Analogously, one obtains an equivariant symplectic Banach–Mazur distance, denoted bydSBMℤ/p​ℤd_{\mathrm{SBM}}^{\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}}, which is obviously no smaller thandSBMd_{\mathrm{SBM}}. Stability results hold in a similar manner. In particular, for the domains(WH(p),λ^)(W_{H^{(p)}},\hat{\lambda})considered in Example1.2, we have the following stability estimate:dSBMℤ/p​ℤ​((WH(p),λ^),(WG(p),λ^))≤dT​(H,G).d_{\mathrm{SBM}}^{\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}}\left((W_{H^{(p)}},\hat{\lambda}),(W_{G^{(p)}},\hat{\lambda})\right)\leq d_{\mathrm{T}}(H,G).

For the lower bound ofdSBMℤ/p​ℤ​((WH(p),λ^),(WG(p),λ^))d_{\mathrm{SBM}}^{\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}}((W_{H^{(p)}},\hat{\lambda}),(W_{G^{(p)}},\hat{\lambda})),
the (strict) Liouville diffeomorphismsρH,p\rho_{H,p}andρG,p\rho_{G,p}in (5) induce aℤ/p​ℤ\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}-action on the corresponding filteredS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology groups. Then, by the general representation theory of persistence modules (see Section 4.4 in[34]), one can extract refined invariants from, for instance, the barcodes of subrepresentations, and use them to provide a lower bound. Further developments in this direction will be presented in[16].

## 1.2.Bounded relative growth rate

Before turning to a different topic, let us relate the Thompson-type metricdTd_{\rm T}in Definition1.3to another classical quantity, the relative growth rate222This subject arose in a discussion with L. Polterovich, who conjectured that a result of the form of TheoremCbelow should hold.. We briefly recall its definition within the framework of contact geometry (see[15]). For a contact manifold(M,ξ)(M,\xi)with a co-oriented contact structure, denote byPCont0⁡(M,ξ)\operatorname{PCont}_{0}(M,\xi)the set of contact isotopies, i.e., the paths (parametrized by[0,1][0,1]) in the contactomorphism group of(M,ξ)(M,\xi)starting fromIdM\operatorname{Id}_{M}. Fix a contact11-formα∈Ω1​(M)\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(M); then each contact isotopyψ={ψt}t∈[0,1]∈PCont0⁡(M,ξ)\psi=\{\psi^{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\in\operatorname{PCont}_{0}(M,\xi)is generated by a unique contact Hamiltonian functionhα​(ψ):[0,1]×M→ℝh_{\alpha}(\psi)\colon[0,1]\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. Explicitly,hα​(ψ)=α​(Xψ)​where​dψdt=Xψ∘ψh_{\alpha}(\psi)=\alpha(X_{\psi})\,\,\,\text{ where }\,\,\,\derivative{\psi}{t}=X_{\psi}\circ\psi

We callψ\psipositive if the correspondinghαh_{\alpha}is pointwise positive.
Denote byPCont+⁡(M,ξ)\operatorname{PCont}_{+}(M,\xi)the set of all positive contact isotopies inPCont0⁡(M,ξ)\operatorname{PCont}_{0}(M,\xi). For brevity, setG≔Cont~0​(M,ξ){\rm G}\coloneqq\widetilde{\rm Cont}_{0}(M,\xi)and consider(13)G+≔{[ψ]∈G∣ψ∈PCont+⁡(M,ξ)}.{\rm G}_{+}\coloneqq\{[\psi]\in{\rm G}\mid\psi\in\operatorname{PCont}_{+}(M,\xi)\}.

Fix any contact11-formα\alphaonMM. For any two elements[φ],[ψ]∈G[\varphi],[\psi]\in{\rm G}, define[φ]⪰[ψ][\varphi]\succeq[\psi]if and only ifhα​(φ)≥hα​(ψ)h_{\alpha}(\varphi)\geq h_{\alpha}(\psi)pointwise for some representatives (still denoted byφ\varphiandψ\psi, without loss of generality) of[φ][\varphi]and[ψ][\psi], respectively (Proposition 1.4.B in[15]). ThenG+{\rm G}_{+}in (13) consists of the dominants ofG{\rm G}, where we calla∈G∖{1}a\in{\rm G}\setminus\{1\}a dominant if for everyb∈Gb\in{\rm G}there exists somek∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}such thatak⪰ba^{k}\succeq b. Fora∈G+a\in{\rm G}_{+}andb∈Gb\in{\rm G}, defineρ⪰+​(a,b)\displaystyle\rho^{+}_{\succeq}(a,b)≔liml→∞inf{k∈ℤ∣ak⪰bl}l\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\inf\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\mid a^{k}\succeq b^{l}\}}{l}=inf{kl∈ℚ|(k∈ℤ,l∈ℕ)​(ak⪰bl)}.\displaystyle=\inf\left.\left\{\frac{k}{l}\in\mathbb{Q}\,\right|\,(k\in\mathbb{Z},l\in\mathbb{N})(a^{k}\succeq b^{l})\right\}.

For the second equality above, see the proof of Theorem 2.4 in[37]. Ifb∈G+b\in{\rm G}_{+}, then one similarly definesρ⪰−​(a,b)≔ρ⪰+​(b,a)\rho_{\succeq}^{-}(a,b)\coloneqq\rho_{\succeq}^{+}(b,a). Therelative growth rate betweenaaandbbis defined byγ⪰​(a,b)≔max⁡{|ρ⪰+​(a,b)|,|ρ⪰−​(a,b)|}.\gamma_{\succeq}(a,b)\coloneqq\max\{|\rho_{\succeq}^{+}(a,b)|,|\rho_{\succeq}^{-}(a,b)|\}.

Moreover, ifa,b∈G+a,b\in{\rm G}_{+}, then bothρ⪰+​(a,b)\rho_{\succeq}^{+}(a,b)andρ⪰−​(a,b)\rho_{\succeq}^{-}(a,b)are nonzero, since inequality (1.1.A) in[15]implies thatρ⪰+​(a,b)​ρ⪰−​(a,b)≥1\rho_{\succeq}^{+}(a,b)\rho_{\succeq}^{-}(a,b)\geq 1. Definedγ:G+×G+→[0,∞)d_{\gamma}\colon{\rm G}_{+}\times{\rm G}_{+}\rightarrow[0,\infty)as follows (see Lemma 1.7.A in[15]and (12) in[36]):(14)dγ​(a,b)≔ln⁡γ⪰​(a,b)​for anya,b∈G+d_{\gamma}(a,b)\coloneqq\ln\gamma_{\succeq}(a,b)\,\,\,\,\mbox{for any $a,b\in{\rm G}_{+}$}

and one easily verifies that(G+,dγ)({\rm G}_{+},d_{\gamma})is a pseudo-metric space.

We now focus on a special family of contact manifolds—prequantization spaces. Let(M,ξ)(M,\xi)be a prequantization space of a closed symplectic manifold(X,ω)(X,\omega). By definition, there exists a principalS1S^{1}-bundle structurep:M→Xp\colon M\rightarrow Xand anS1S^{1}-invariant11-formα∈Ω1​(M)\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(M)such thatd​α=p∗​ωd\alpha=p^{*}\omega. This setting is particularly useful for linking symplectic Hamiltonian dynamics with contact Hamiltonian dynamics. Explicitly, given a HamiltonianH:[0,1]×X→ℝH\colon[0,1]\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}, there exists a contact isotopyψH={ψHt}t∈[0,1]∈PCont0​(M,ξ)\psi_{H}=\{\psi_{H}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\in{\rm PCont}_{0}(M,\xi), generated byp∗​H:[0,1]×M→ℝp^{*}H\colon[0,1]\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. IfHHis positive, then so isp∗​Hp^{*}H, which yields a positive contact isotopyψH∈PCont+​(M,ξ)\psi_{H}\in{\rm PCont}_{+}(M,\xi)and a class[ψH]∈G+[\psi_{H}]\in{\rm G}_{+}(see (13)).

Letℋ+​(X)≔{smooth​H:X→ℝ+}\mathcal{H}_{+}(X)\coloneqq\{\text{ smooth }H\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\}. Then, in the same spirit as Definition1.3, we can define another Thompson-type metric onℋ+​(X)\mathcal{H}_{+}(X)as follows:dT,p​(H,G)≔inf{ln⁡C>0|∃φ∈Ham⁡(X,ω)​s.t.1C​H≤G∘φ≤C​H}d_{\rm T,p}(H,G)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\ln C>0\,\left|\,\exists\,\varphi\in\operatorname{Ham}(X,\omega)\,\,{\rm{s.t.}}\,\,\frac{1}{C}H\leq G\circ\varphi\leq CH\right\}\right.

for anyH,G∈ℋ+​(X)H,G\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(X), where the “p{\rm p}” indT,pd_{\rm T,p}indicates that we are working in the prequantization setting. The verification thatdT,pd_{\rm T,p}is a pseudo-metric follows in the same way as part (2) of Proposition3.1. With respect to the pseudo-metricdγd_{\gamma}defined in (14), we then have the following comparison.

## Theorem C.

Given a prequantization(M,ξ)→(X,ω)(M,\xi)\to(X,\omega), we havedγ​([ψH],[ψG])≤dT,p​(H,G)d_{\gamma}([\psi_{H}],[\psi_{G}])\leq d_{\rm T,p}(H,G)

for anyH,G∈ℋ+​(X)H,G\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(X).

Note that TheoremCpartially addresses Problem 1.2.B in[15]—specifically in the prequantization setting—concerning the estimation of the relative growth rate in geometric or dynamical terms.

## Remark 1.11.

Note that the valuedγ​(⋅,⋅)d_{\gamma}(\cdot,\cdot)is not directly computable, as its inputs are homotopy classes. Therefore, it is nontrivial to obtain large-scale geometric properties of(G+,dγ)({\rm G}_{+},d_{\gamma})(and hence of(Ham​(X,ω),dT,p)({\rm Ham}(X,\omega),d_{\rm T,p})). For instance, a meaningful question is the following: for(X,ω)=(S2,ωstd)(X,\omega)=(S^{2},\omega_{\rm std})and(M,ξ)=(S3,ξstd)(M,\xi)=(S^{3},\xi_{\rm std}), how candT,pd_{\rm T,p}anddγd_{\gamma}be estimated? We plan to address this question systematically somewhere else.

## 1.3.Lifted topological entropy

We next turn to a different topic: investigating the relationship between the topological entropies of various dynamics under the tube construction (2) with condition (3). This will illustrate, from a quantitative perspective, how the dynamical systems involved change.

Recall that, on a fixed compact metric space(X,d)(X,d)and for any continuous flowφ:ℝ×X→X\varphi:\mathbb{R}\times X\to X, itstopological entropyis defined by(15)htop​(φ)=limε→0lim supT→∞1T​ln⁡Nsep​(φ,T,ε)h_{\text{top}}(\varphi)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\ln N_{\text{sep}}(\varphi,T,\varepsilon)

whereNsep​(φ,T,ε)N_{\text{sep}}(\varphi,T,\varepsilon)denotes the maximal cardinality of subsets in which any two distinct pointsx,yx,ysatisfymax0≤t≤T⁡d​(φ​(t,x),φ​(t,y))≥ε\max_{0\leq t\leq T}d(\varphi(t,x),\varphi(t,y))\geq\varepsilon. In other words, no two distinct points in such a subset have orbits under the flowφ​(t,⋅)\varphi(t,\cdot)that remainε\varepsilon-close up to timeTT. Note that, by Proposition 3.1.2 of[25], the topological entropies computed with respect to different metrics onXXcoincide; hence we have suppressed the metric in the notationhtop​(φ)h_{\mathrm{top}}(\varphi).

There are two variants of the notationhtop​(φ)h_{\rm top}(\varphi). (i) Whenφ\varphiis a continuous map onXX(without time-parameter), one defineshtop​(φ)h_{\rm top}(\varphi)in a parallel way to (15), by simply replacingφ​(t,x)\varphi(t,x)with the iteratesφk​(x)\varphi^{k}(x)and1T\frac{1}{T}with1k\frac{1}{k}. The resulting value is still denoted byhtop​(φ)h_{\rm top}(\varphi). (ii) For any flowφ:ℝ×X→X\varphi:\mathbb{R}\times X\to X, by rescaling the time-parameter one shows thathtop​(φ)=htop​(φ1)h_{\rm top}(\varphi)=h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}), whereφ1≔φ​(1,⋅)\varphi^{1}\coloneqq\varphi(1,\cdot)is the time-1 map of the flowφ\varphi. For a comprehensive treatment of the basic properties of topological entropy, we refer the reader to the book[25]. Although topological entropy has been studied extensively, in this paper we focus on the following settings.

Given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), recall the set of Hamiltonian functionsℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)defined in (6). On the one hand, one can consider the topological entropy of the time-1 map of the Hamiltonian flowφHt\varphi_{H}^{t}on(W,λ)(W,\lambda), namelyhtop​(φH1)h_{\rm top}(\varphi_{H}^{1}); on the other hand, under the tube construction (2), one can considerhtop​(φλ^|∂WH1)​i.e., topological entropy of the Reeb flow on∂(WH,λ^).h_{\rm top}\left(\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\right)\,\,\,\,\mbox{i.e., topological entropy of the Reeb flow on $\partial(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})$}.

Corollary2.8gives an explicit relation between the Hamiltonian flow on(W,d​λ)(W,d\lambda)and the Reeb flow on∂(WH,λ^)\partial(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}). Combining this with Proposition6.1, we obtain the following comparison theorem.

## Theorem D.

Given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), for anyH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), we have(minS1×W⁡τλ,H)​htop​(φλ^|∂WH1)≤htop​(φH1)≤(maxS1×W⁡τλ,H)​htop​(φλ^|∂WH1)\left(\min_{S^{1}\times W}\tau_{\lambda,H}\right)h_{\rm top}\left(\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\right)\leq h_{\rm top}(\varphi_{H}^{1})\leq\left(\max_{S^{1}\times W}\tau_{\lambda,H}\right)h_{\rm top}\left(\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\right)

whereτλ,H\tau_{\lambda,H}is the positive function defined in (3).

An immediate consequence is the following.

## Corollary 1.12.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain withhtop​(φλ|∂W1)>0h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{\lambda|_{\partial W}})>0, then for anyH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), we havehtop​(φλ^|∂WH1)>0​and thenhtop​(φH1)>0.h_{\rm top}\left(\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\right)>0\,\,\,\,\mbox{and then $h_{\rm top}(\varphi_{H}^{1})>0$}.

## Proof.

By the discussion in Section2.2, we haveφλ^|∂WH1:∂W×{0}→∂W×{0},\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}:\partial W\times\{0\}\to\partial W\times\{0\},

where∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}sits inside a distinguished componentW×{0}W\times\{0\}of∂(WH,λ^)\partial(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}). In other words,∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}is an invariant subset ofφλ^|∂WH1\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}. Moreover, sinceλ^|∂W×{0}=λ|∂W\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W\times\{0\}}=\lambda|_{\partial W}, we know thatφλ^|∂WH1|∂W×{0}=φλ|∂W1.\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\big|_{\partial W\times\{0\}}=\varphi^{1}_{{\lambda}|_{\partial W}}.

Hence, by our hypothesis,htop​(φλ^|∂WH1)≥htop​(φλ^|∂WH1|∂W×{0})=htop​(φλ|∂W1)>0.h_{\rm top}\left(\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\right)\geq h_{\rm top}\left(\varphi^{1}_{\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}}\big|_{\partial W\times\{0\}}\right)=h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{\lambda|_{\partial W}})>0.

Thenhtop​(φH1)>0h_{\rm top}(\varphi_{H}^{1})>0by TheoremD, which completes the proof.
∎

## Remark 1.13.

When(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is of dimension 4 (so that∂(W,λ)\partial(W,\lambda)is of dimension 3), the positive-entropy hypothesis can be ensured in a generic sense (see Theorem 1.5 in[10]).

## Remark 1.14.

WhendimW=2\dim W=2, there is a classical way to obtain stability of the Hamiltonian entropy. Indeed, ifHj→HH_{j}\to HinC∞​(S1×W)C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times W)withφHj1→φH1\varphi^{1}_{H_{j}}\to\varphi^{1}_{H}in theC∞C^{\infty}topology, the mapf↦htop​(f)f\mapsto h_{\mathrm{top}}(f)is continuous on the space ofC∞C^{\infty}diffeomorphisms. More precisely, the upper semicontinuity in theC∞C^{\infty}case is obtained by Yomdin’s theory via uniform control of local entropy, see Section 4.3.1 in[8]. On the other hand, Katok’s horseshoe theorem forC1+αC^{1+\alpha}surface diffeomorphisms gives lower semicontinuity: positive entropy can be approximated by uniformly hyperbolic horseshoes, and such horseshoes persist underC1C^{1}perturbations[24]. Hence, for two-dimensionalWW,H↦htop​(φH1)H\mapsto h_{\mathrm{top}}(\varphi^{1}_{H})is continuous with respect to theC∞C^{\infty}topology on Hamiltonians.

By[3], for any closed contact33-manifold(Y,ξ)(Y,\xi), there exists aC∞C^{\infty}-generic open subset𝒱0​(Y,ξ)\mathcal{V}_{0}(Y,\xi)of contact form such that the topological entropy is lower semi-continuous at every point of this subset with respect to theC0C^{0}-distance. We can use this result to obtain the stability properties of topological entropy with respect to the Thompson metric.

## Example 1.17.

Given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda)and an autonomousH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), set((WH)0,λ0)=(W,λ)​and​((WH)1,λ1)=(WH,λ^).\left((W_{H})_{0},\lambda_{0}\right)=(W,\lambda)\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\left((W_{H})_{1},\lambda_{1}\right)=(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}).

Then forn≥2n\geq 2, given Liouville domain((WH)n−1,λn−1)\left((W_{H})_{n-1},\lambda_{n-1}\right), define(WH)n≔{((w,z1,…,zn−1),zn)∈(WH)n−1×ℂ|π​|zn|2≤H​(w)−π​∑i=1n−1|zi|2}(W_{H})_{n}\coloneqq\left\{((w,z_{1},...,z_{n-1}),z_{n})\in(W_{H})_{n-1}\times\mathbb{C}\,\bigg|\,\pi|z_{n}|^{2}\leq H(w)-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|z_{i}|^{2}\right\}

andλn≔λn−1+12​rn2​d​θn\lambda_{n}\coloneqq\lambda_{n-1}+\frac{1}{2}r_{n}^{2}d\theta_{n}, wherezn=rn​e−1​θnz_{n}=r_{n}e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta_{n}}. Unfolding the recursion, we obtain(WH)n={(w,z1,…,zn)∈W×ℂn|π​∑i=1n|zi|2≤H​(w)}(W_{H})_{n}=\Bigl\{(w,z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in W\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\;\Big|\;\pi\sum_{i=1}^{n}|z_{i}|^{2}\leq H(w)\Bigr\}

andλn=λ+12​∑i=1nri2​d​θi\lambda_{n}=\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{2}\,d\theta_{i}. For simplicity, denote by(αH)n≔λn|∂(WH)n(\alpha_{H})_{n}\coloneqq\lambda_{n}|_{\partial(W_{H})_{n}}the induced contact 1-form on the contact boundary∂((WH)n,λn)\partial((W_{H})_{n},\lambda_{n}).

By examining the Reeb dynamics on∂((WH)n,λn)\partial((W_{H})_{n},\lambda_{n}), we obtain the following conclusion, which is partially due to the fact that each “lifting” from(WH)i(W_{H})_{i}to(WH)i+1(W_{H})_{i+1}only produces, dynamically, a rotation in the newly added coordinate, and such a rotation has zero topological entropy.

## Corollary 1.18.

Given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda)and an autonomousH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), for anyn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, we have(minW⁡τλ,H)​htop​(φ(αH)n1)≤htop​(φH1)≤(maxW⁡τλ,H)​htop​(φ(αH)n1).\left(\min_{W}\tau_{\lambda,H}\right)h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{(\alpha_{H})_{n}})\leq h_{\rm top}(\varphi_{H}^{1})\leq\left(\max_{W}\tau_{\lambda,H}\right)h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{(\alpha_{H})_{n}}).

Here,φ(αH)n1\varphi^{1}_{(\alpha_{H})_{n}}is the time-1 map of the Reeb flow on∂((WH)n,λn)\partial((W_{H})_{n},\lambda_{n}). Moreover, ifhtop​(φλ|∂W1)>0h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{\lambda|_{\partial W}})>0, thenhtop​(φ(αH)n1)>0h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{(\alpha_{H})_{n}})>0for everyn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.

Finally, for any two-dimensional(W,λ)(W,\lambda), by[24], positive entropy in two-dimensional dynamics implies the existence of transverse homoclinic points, which cannot arise in the integrable setting (which is essentially equivalent to being autonomous). Hence, for any autonomous Hamiltonian dynamics in this setting, the topological entropy is zero. As a direct implication of Corollary1.18, we obtain the following result, which is opposite to Corollary1.12, as well as the second conclusion of Corollary1.18.

## Corollary 1.19.

LetH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)be an autonomous Hamiltonian on any two-dimensional Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda). Thenhtop​(φ(αH)n1)=0h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{(\alpha_{H})_{n}})=0for everyn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.

To this end, recall that a flowφ​(t,⋅)\varphi(t,\cdot)on a metric space is calledequicontinuousif, for everyε>0\varepsilon>0, there existsδ>0\delta>0such thatd​(x,y)<δd(x,y)<\deltaimpliesd​(φ​(t,x),φ​(t,y))<εd(\varphi(t,x),\varphi(t,y))<\varepsilonfor allt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}. It is straightforward to verify from the definition that any equicontinuous flow has zero topological entropy (for completeness, a proof will be provided in Proposition2.14in Section2.5). Althoughhtop​(φ(αH)n1)=0h_{\rm top}(\varphi^{1}_{(\alpha_{H})_{n}})=0for any autonomousH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)on a two-dimensional Liouville domain and anyn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, there exists an example in which the Reeb flow of(αH)n(\alpha_{H})_{n}is not equicontinuous.

## Example 1.20.

Let(W,λ)=(B¯2​(π),λstd=12​r2​d​φ)(W,\lambda)=(\overline{B}^{\,2}(\pi),\lambda_{\rm std}=\frac{1}{2}r^{2}d\varphi)and letH​(z)=e−e|z|2H(z)=e-e^{|z|^{2}}, or equivalentlyH​(r)=e−er2H(r)=e-e^{r^{2}}.
