The verification of Example1.20will be postponed to Section2.5.

## 1.4.C0C^{0}-Liouville domains

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain. Define the followingC0C^{0}-strong class of Hamiltonian functions on(W,λ)(W,\lambda):ℋ0(W,λ)≔{H∈C0(S1×W,[0,∞))|H|S1×∂W=0,H>0onS1×Int(W),\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda)\coloneqq\left\{H\in C^{0}(S^{1}\times W,[0,\infty))\;\middle|\;H|_{S^{1}\times\partial W}=0,\;H>0\text{ on }S^{1}\times{\rm Int}(W),\right.and for everyε>0,cH(ε)≔min(t,w)∈S1×W(H(t,ℒλ−ε(w))−e−εH(t,w))>0}.\displaystyle\left.\text{and for every }\varepsilon>0,\ c_{H}(\varepsilon)\coloneqq\min_{(t,w)\in S^{1}\times W}(H(t,\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{-\varepsilon}(w))-e^{-\varepsilon}H(t,w))>0\right\}.

This is the natural time-dependentC0C^{0}-analogue of the positivity conditionτλ,H>0\tau_{\lambda,H}>0. In particular, every smoothH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)belongs toℋ0​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda)(see Proposition7.1). ForH∈ℋ0​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda), similarly to the tube construction in (2), setWH≔{(w,z)∈W×ℂ|z=r​e2​π​i​t,π​|z|2≤H​(t,w)}W_{H}\coloneqq\left\{(w,z)\in W\times\mathbb{C}\;\middle|\;z=re^{2\pi it},\ \pi|z|^{2}\leq H(t,w)\right\}

and letUH≔Int​(WH)U_{H}\coloneqq{\rm Int}(W_{H}). As before,UHU_{H}carries the exact symplectic formd​λ^d\hat{\lambda}, whereλ^≔λ+π​r2​d​t\hat{\lambda}\coloneqq\lambda+\pi r^{2}\,dt. It is then natural to introduce a symplectic Banach–Mazur distance for (open) interior of Liouville domains.

## Definition 1.21.

LetH,G∈ℋ0​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda). DefinedSBM(WH,WG)≔inf{lnC>0|∃iH,G:1CUH↪UG,s.t.1C2UG⊂iH,G(1CUH)⊂UG∃iG,H:1CUG↪UH,s.t.1C2UH⊂iG,H(1CUG)⊂UH}.d_{\rm SBM}\left(W_{H},W_{G}\right)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\ln C>0\,\left|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\exists i_{H,G}\colon\frac{1}{C}U_{H}{\hookrightarrow}U_{G},{\rm{s.t.}}\,\frac{1}{C^{2}}U_{G}\subset i_{H,G}\left(\frac{1}{C}U_{H}\right)\subset U_{G}\\
\exists i_{G,H}\colon\frac{1}{C}U_{G}{\hookrightarrow}U_{H},{\rm{s.t.}}\,\frac{1}{C^{2}}U_{H}\subset i_{G,H}\left(\frac{1}{C}U_{G}\right)\subset U_{H}\end{array}\right\}\right..\vskip 5.69054pt

Here, forC≥1C\geq 1, we write1C​UH≔ℒλ^−ln⁡C​(UH).\frac{1}{C}U_{H}\coloneqq\mathscr{L}_{\hat{\lambda}}^{-\ln C}(U_{H}).

For a Liouville domain(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W}), one can associate a barcodeℬ​(W,λW)\mathcal{B}(W,\lambda_{W})viaS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology. Usher proved that, for any two Liouville domains(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W})and(V,λV)(V,\lambda_{V}),dbot​(ℬ​(W,λW),ℬ​(V,λV))≤dSBM​((W,λW),(V,λV)).d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B}(W,\lambda_{W}),\mathcal{B}(V,\lambda_{V}))\leq d_{\rm SBM}((W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V})).We next extend this estimate to theC0C^{0}-setting.

## Theorem E.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain. Then the assignmentℋ​(W,λ)→{barcodes}byH↦ℬ​(H)≔ℬ​(WH,λ^)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)\to\{\text{barcodes}\}\quad\mbox{by}\quad H\mapsto\mathcal{B}(H)\coloneqq\mathcal{B}(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})

extends uniquely to a mapℬ:ℋ0​(W,λ)→{barcodes}\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda)\to\{\text{barcodes}\}such thatdbot​(ℬ​(H),ℬ​(G))≤dSBM​(WH,WG)d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B}(H),\mathcal{B}(G))\leq d_{\rm SBM}(W_{H},W_{G})for allH,G∈ℋ0​(W,λ).H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda).In particular, the extended barcode map is continuous with respect todSBMd_{\rm SBM}, and henceC0C^{0}-continuous.

## Remark 1.22.

TheoremEmay be viewed as an application of Usher’s theory of barcodes for open Liouville domains[40]. The Liouville vector field ofλ^=λ+12​r2​d​θ\hat{\lambda}=\lambda+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}d\thetaisY^=Yλ+12​r​∂r\widehat{Y}=Y_{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}, and thereforeℒλ^−s​(w,z)=(ℒλ−s​(w),e−s/2​z)\mathscr{L}_{\hat{\lambda}}^{-s}(w,z)=(\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{-s}(w),e^{-s/2}z). The conditioncH​(s)>0c_{H}(s)>0impliesℒλ^−s​(UH)⊂UH\mathscr{L}_{\hat{\lambda}}^{-s}(U_{H})\subset U_{H}and, fors>0s>0, gives a uniform collar separatingℒλ^−s​(UH)\mathscr{L}_{\hat{\lambda}}^{-s}(U_{H})from∂UH\partial U_{H}. Thus(UH,λ^)(U_{H},\hat{\lambda})is an open Liouville domain in Usher’s sense. The barcodeℬ​(H)\mathcal{B}(H)is therefore the barcode of(UH,λ^)(U_{H},\hat{\lambda}), and for smoothHHit agrees with the barcode of the compact Liouville domain(WH,λ^)(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}). The inequalitydbot​(ℬ​(H),ℬ​(G))≤dSBM​(WH,WG)d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B}(H),\mathcal{B}(G))\leq d_{\rm SBM}(W_{H},W_{G})is exactly Usher’s stability estimate for the fine symplectic Banach–Mazur distance.

## 1.5.Capacity under stabilization

The next topic concerns capacity. Recall that a symplectic capacity is a functionalccfrom the space of symplectic manifolds to the non-negative real numbers, satisfying certain axioms. In particular, the (ball-)normalization axiom states thatc​(B2​n​(a))=c​(Z2​n​(a))=ac(B^{2n}(a))=c(Z^{2n}(a))=a

where, throughout the paper,B2​n​(a)≔{z∈ℂn|π​∑j=1n|zj|2<a},B¯2​n​(a)≔{z∈ℂn|π​∑j=1n|zj|2≤a},B^{2n}(a)\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\;\middle|\;\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}|z_{j}|^{2}<a\right\},\qquad\overline{B}^{\,2n}(a)\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\;\middle|\;\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}|z_{j}|^{2}\leq a\right\},

andZ2​n​(a)≔B2​(a)×ℂn−1Z^{2n}(a)\coloneqq B^{2}(a)\times\mathbb{C}^{n-1}. This axiom excludes volume from being a symplectic capacity. Moreover, the existence of such a (ball-normalized) symplectic capacity is equivalent to Gromov’s celebrated non-squeezing theorem[17]. This leads to the following (first) symplectic capacity:(16)wG​(M,ω)≔sup{a|∃a symplectic embedding(B2​n​(a),ωstd)↪(M,ω)}.w_{G}(M,\omega)\coloneqq\sup\{a\,|\,\mbox{$\exists$ a symplectic embedding $(B^{2n}(a),\omega_{\rm std})\hookrightarrow(M,\omega)$}\}.

commonly known as the Gromov width.

In what follows, we investigate howwGw_{G}behaves under the tube construction (2), via input Hamiltonian functions in the set (6).

## Lemma 1.23.

For any Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), the mapℋ0​(W,λ)→[0,∞),H↦wG​(WH,d​λ^)\mathcal{H}^{0}(W,\lambda)\to[0,\infty),\qquad H\mapsto w_{G}(W_{H},d\hat{\lambda})

is continuous with respect to theC0C^{0}-topology.

## Remark 1.24.

Although the target(M,ω)(M,\omega)in (16) is meant to be a symplectic manifold, the definition can be modified so that(B2​n​(a),ωstd)↪(Int​(M),ω)(B^{2n}(a),\omega_{\rm std})\hookrightarrow({\rm Int}(M),\omega)whenMMhas boundary, or even when its boundary is not smooth. For convenience, we still denotewG​(Int​(M),ω)w_{G}({\rm Int}(M),\omega)bywG​(M,ω)w_{G}(M,\omega).

In particular, this implies that, for any fixed(W,λ)(W,\lambda)andH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda),wG​(Wa​H,d​λ^)w_{G}(W_{aH},d\hat{\lambda})tends to zero asa→0a\to 0. As another main result of this paper, the following theorem establishes certain asymptotic behaviors ofwGw_{G}.

## Theorem F.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain, and letH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)be possibly time-dependent. SetM≔max(t,w)∈S1×W⁡H​(t,w),H¯​(w)≔∫01H​(t,w)​𝑑t,M¯≔maxw∈W⁡H¯​(w)M\coloneqq\max_{(t,w)\in S^{1}\times W}H(t,w),\overline{H}(w)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}H(t,w)\,dt,\overline{M}\coloneqq\max_{w\in W}\overline{H}(w).
Then we have the following asymptotic behaviors:
- (i)

M¯≤lim infa→0+wG​(Wa​H,d​λ^)a≤lim supa→0+wG​(Wa​H,d​λ^)a≤M\overline{M}\leq\liminf_{a\to 0^{+}}\frac{w_{G}(W_{aH},d\hat{\lambda})}{a}\leq\limsup_{a\to 0^{+}}\frac{w_{G}(W_{aH},d\hat{\lambda})}{a}\leq M.
- (ii)

lima→∞wG​(Wa​H,d​λ^)=wG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)\lim_{a\to\infty}w_{G}(W_{aH},d\hat{\lambda})=w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod}).

In particular, ifHHis autonomous, thenH¯=H\overline{H}=H, hencelima→0+wG​(Wa​H,d​λ^)a=maxw∈W⁡H​(w)\lim_{a\to 0^{+}}\frac{w_{G}(W_{aH},d\hat{\lambda})}{a}=\max_{w\in W}H(w).

Symplectic manifolds of the formW×ℂW\times\mathbb{C}have been studied before. WhenWWis a standard (four-dimensional) toric domain,W×ℂW\times\mathbb{C}(or more generallyW×ℂNW\times\mathbb{C}^{N}) provides the so-calledstabilized structure, and the stabilized embedding problem—the existence of symplectic embeddings between stabilized domains—is a rich subject (see[11]). Here, part (ii) of TheoremFaddresses a different problem: the source is a higher-dimensional symplectic ball rather than a stabilized ball. When(M,ω)(M,\omega)is a closed symplectic manifold—in particular, whenM=𝕋2M=\mathbb{T}^{2}—earlier results on estimating the Gromov width ofM×ℂM\times\mathbb{C}appear in[18]. Interestingly, within the class of symplectic manifolds (with or without boundary), the value ofwGw_{G}may be either finite or infinite, depending on the input. We illustrate this via the following two propositions.

Recall thatcZ​(W,d​λ)c_{Z}(W,d\lambda)denotes the cylindrical capacity of the Liouville domain(W,d​λ)(W,d\lambda), measuring the smallest symplectic cylinderZ2​n​(a)Z^{2n}(a)into which(W,d​λ)(W,d\lambda)can be symplectically embedded.

## Proposition 1.25.

The following list collects several cases of Liouville domains(W,λ)(W,\lambda)whose stabilizations have finite Gromov width.
- (i)

If(W,λ)(W,\lambda)satisfieswG​(W,d​λ)=cZ​(W,d​λ)w_{G}(W,d\lambda)=c_{Z}(W,d\lambda), thenwG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)=wG​(W,d​λ)w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})=w_{G}(W,d\lambda).
- (ii)

If(W,λ)=(S2∖⋃j=1nInt​(Dj2),λ)(W,\lambda)=(S^{2}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}{\rm Int}(D_{j}^{2}),\lambda), whereD12,⋯,Dn2⊂S2D_{1}^{2},\cdots,D_{n}^{2}\subset S^{2}are pairwise disjoint smoothly embedded closed disks andn≥1n\geq 1, thenwG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)=∫Wωw_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})=\int_{W}\omega.
- (iii)

If(W,λ)=(Dg∗​N,λcan)(W,\lambda)=(D_{g}^{*}N,\lambda_{\rm can})whereNNis a connected closed surface which is not an oriented surface of genus at least22, thenwG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)<∞w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})<\infty.

## Example 1.26.

By[11], standard examples satisfying part (i) of Proposition1.25are the monotone toric domains in(ℝ2​n,ωstd)(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{\rm std}).

## Remark 1.27.

(1) The inequalitieswG​(W,d​λ)≤wG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)w_{G}(W,d\lambda)\leq w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})andcZ​(W,d​λ)≥cZ​(W×ℂ,ωprod)c_{Z}(W,d\lambda)\geq c_{Z}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})hold by definition. HencewG​(W,d​λ)=cZ​(W,d​λ)w_{G}(W,d\lambda)=c_{Z}(W,d\lambda)implieswG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)=cZ​(W×ℂ,ωprod)w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})=c_{Z}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod}). (2) Conclusion (ii) of Proposition1.25also holds forS2S^{2}(without boundary). (3) A symplectically uniruled symplectic44-manifold (see Section2.6) admitsnoclosed Lagrangian submanifold of genusg≥2g\geq 2; see Theorem 2.2 of[41]and Theorem 7.3 of[42]. Thus part (iii) of Proposition1.25is consistent with the expectation that oriented surfaces of genus at least22should exhibit different behavior.

## Proposition 1.28.

Let(X,ωX)(X,\omega_{X})be a symplectic manifold such thatwG​(X,ωX)=∞.w_{G}(X,\omega_{X})=\infty.Let(Σ,ωΣ)(\Sigma,\omega_{\Sigma})be a symplectic surface of positive genus (with or without boundary). ThenwG​(Σ×X,ωΣ⊕ωX)=∞.w_{G}(\Sigma\times X,\omega_{\Sigma}\oplus\omega_{X})=\infty.

As an immediate consequence, we obtain the following.

## Corollary 1.29.

LetW=Σ1×⋯×ΣnW=\Sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n}, whereΣi\Sigma_{i}is a two dimensional surface of positive genus (with or without boundary), thenwG​(W×ℂ,ωprod)=∞w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\rm prod})=\infty.

## 1.6.Large-scale geometry

In this subsection, we show that the Thompson-type metric on Hamiltonians already exhibits nontrivial large-scale geometry, even on the two-dimensional disk. The key input comes from[12], where the authors constructed a family of smooth concave toric domains inℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}whose Banach–Mazur distances grow linearly with theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}-distance of the parameter.

Our task is to transfer that construction to the Hamiltonian setting considered in this paper. The key observation is that the toric domains from[12]can be expressed as graph-type domains over a fixed interval. This makes it possible, after a simple rescaling, to realize them as tube domains associated to explicit radial Hamiltonians on the 2-diskB¯2​(a)\overline{B}^{\,2}(a). Once this is done, TheoremAconverts the Banach–Mazur lower bound for those domains into a lower bound for the Thompson-type metricdTd_{\rm T}from Definition1.3.

We now recall the precise geometric input that will be used.

## Proposition 1.30(Section 2.4 in[12]).

FixN≥1N\geq 1. Then there exist constantscN>0c_{N}>0and a family of smooth concave toric domains{Xv}v∈ℝN⊂ℂ2\{X_{v}\}_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}such thatdBM​(Xv,Xw)≥cN​‖v−w‖∞d_{\rm BM}(X_{v},X_{w})\geq c_{N}\|v-w\|_{\infty}

for anyv,w∈ℝNv,w\in\mathbb{R}^{N}. In particular, for eachv∈ℝNv\in\mathbb{R}^{N}, there exists a smooth concave functionfv:[0,1]→[0,∞)f_{v}:[0,1]\to[0,\infty)such thatfv​(1)=0f_{v}(1)=0andXv={(u,z)∈ℂ2∣π​|u|2≤1,π​|z|2≤fv​(π​|u|2)}X_{v}=\{(u,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid\pi|u|^{2}\leq 1,\ \pi|z|^{2}\leq f_{v}(\pi|u|^{2})\}.

Based on the family of domains constructed in Proposition1.30, we are able to construct explicit Hamiltonians on the disk whose pairwisedTd_{\rm T}-distances are bounded below by theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}-distance of the parameters.

## Theorem G.

For any fixeda>0a>0andN≥1N\geq 1, there exist a mapΦN,a:ℝN→ℋ+​(B¯2​(a),λstd)\Phi_{N,a}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathcal{H}^{+}(\overline{B}^{\,2}(a),\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}),v↦ΦN,a​(v)v\mapsto\Phi_{N,a}(v)and a constantcN>0c_{N}>0, depending only onNN, such thatdT​(ΦN,a​(v),ΦN,a​(w))≥cN​‖v−w‖∞d_{\rm T}(\Phi_{N,a}(v),\Phi_{N,a}(w))\geq c_{N}\|v-w\|_{\infty}

for allv,w∈ℝNv,w\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

The goal in this subsection is to import the large-scale geometry of the toric domains from Proposition1.30into the Hamiltonian space on a fixed disk. For each parameterv∈ℝNv\in\mathbb{R}^{N}, the concave toric domainXvX_{v}is determined by a profile functionfvf_{v}. After rescaling this profile to[0,a][0,a], we define a radial HamiltonianHvH_{v}onB¯2​(a)\overline{B}^{\,2}(a)byHv​(u)≔f~v​(π​|u|2)H_{v}(u)\coloneqq\widetilde{f}_{v}(\pi|u|^{2}). Then the associated tube domain is exactly the Liouville rescaling of the toric domain, namelyU​(Hv)=a​Xv{\rm U}(H_{v})=aX_{v}. Thus the Banach–Mazur lower bound for the family{Xv}\{X_{v}\}becomes a lower bound for the symplectic Banach–Mazur distance between the tube domains. Finally, TheoremAconverts this domain-level lower bound into the desired lower bound for the Thompson-type metricdTd_{\rm T}on Hamiltonians.

Note that, although the function spaceℋ+​(B¯2​(a),λstd)\mathcal{H}^{+}(\overline{B}^{\,2}(a),\lambda_{\mathrm{std}})tends to be large itself, due to the group action ofSymp​(B¯2​(a),λstd){\rm Symp}(\overline{B}^{\,2}(a),\lambda_{\rm std})in Definition1.3, it is not immediately clear whetherdTd_{\rm T}retains enough freedom. TheoremGconfirms that(ℋ+​(B¯2​(a),λstd),dT)(\mathcal{H}^{+}(\overline{B}^{\,2}(a),\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}),d_{\rm T})is at least as large as any high-dimensional Euclidean space(ℝN,∥⋅∥∞)(\mathbb{R}^{N},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}). The construction (and the estimate ofdBMd_{\rm BM}) in Proposition1.30is based on ECH capacities and their Weyl law, and is therefore restricted to four-dimensional domains. This explains why, in TheoremG, we can establish the large-scale geometry only onB¯2​(a)\overline{B}^{\,2}(a). It would be interesting to explore whether the conclusion of TheoremGextends to higher-dimensional base Liouville domains(W,λ)(W,\lambda).

## 1.7.Systolic ratio estimation

Given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), denote bySys⁡(∂W)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W)the systole of the contact boundary(∂W,λ|∂W)(\partial W,\lambda|_{\partial W}), that is, the minimal period of a closed Reeb orbit ofλ|∂W\lambda|_{\partial W}. Similarly, forH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), denote bySys⁡(∂WH)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})the systole of(∂WH,αH)(\partial W_{H},\alpha_{H}). Recall the defining propertyτλ,H(=ιXH​λ+H)>0\tau_{\lambda,H}(=\iota_{X_{H}}\lambda+H)>0from (3), and setτmin​(H)≔minS1×W⁡τλ,H\tau_{\min}(H)\coloneqq\min_{S^{1}\times W}\tau_{\lambda,H}. Recall also that the normalized contact volume of(∂WH,αH)(\partial W_{H},\alpha_{H})is defined byVol⁡(∂WH,αH)≔1n!​∫∂WHαH∧(d​αH)n.\operatorname{Vol}(\partial W_{H},\alpha_{H})\coloneqq\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\partial W_{H}}\alpha_{H}\wedge(d\alpha_{H})^{n}.

The systolic ratio ofWHW_{H}is defined asρ​(WH)≔Sys(∂WH)n+1Vol⁡(∂WH,αH).\rho(W_{H})\coloneqq\frac{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})^{n+1}}{\operatorname{Vol}(\partial W_{H},\alpha_{H})}.

We then have the following estimate, whose proof is given here, as it is fairly short and can be regarded as a direct consequence of Corollary2.10.

## Proposition 1.31.

Let(W2​n,λ)(W^{2n},\lambda)be a2​n2n-dimensional Liouville domain. Then, for anyH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda),n!min{Sys(∂W),τmin(H)}n+1(n+1)​Cal⁡(H)≤ρ​(WH)≤n!Sys(∂W)n+1(n+1)​Cal⁡(H).\frac{n!\,\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\min}(H)\}^{n+1}}{(n+1)\operatorname{Cal}(H)}\leq\rho(W_{H})\leq\frac{n!\,\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W)^{n+1}}{(n+1)\operatorname{Cal}(H)}.

## Proof.

By Corollary2.10, the closed Reeb orbits on∂WH\partial W_{H}are of two kinds. First, the invariant subset∂W×{0}⊂∂WH\partial W\times\{0\}\subset\partial W_{H}carries exactly the original Reeb flow, so the closed Reeb orbits there are precisely those on∂W\partial W; in particular, their periods are bounded below bySys⁡(∂W)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W). Second, the Reeb orbits arising from integer-periodic Hamiltonian orbits ofHHhave periodsTt0,w0,k=∫0kτλ,H​(t0+σ,φHσ​(t0,w0))​𝑑σ.T_{t_{0},w_{0},k}=\int_{0}^{k}\tau_{\lambda,H}(t_{0}+\sigma,\varphi_{H}^{\sigma}(t_{0},w_{0}))\,d\sigma.Hence these periods are bounded below byτmin​(H)\tau_{\min}(H). ThereforeSys⁡(∂WH)≥min⁡{Sys⁡(∂W),τmin​(H)}\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\geq\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\min}(H)\}. On the other hand, since∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}is an invariant subset of∂WH\partial W_{H}, we also haveSys⁡(∂WH)≤Sys⁡(∂W)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\leq\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W).

It remains to compute the normalized contact volume. By Stokes’ theorem,∫∂WHαH∧(d​αH)n=∫WH(d​λ^)n+1\int_{\partial W_{H}}\alpha_{H}\wedge(d\alpha_{H})^{n}=\int_{W_{H}}(d\hat{\lambda})^{n+1}. ThereforeVol⁡(∂WH,αH)=1n!​∫WH(d​λ^)n+1=(n+1)​Vol⁡(WH)\operatorname{Vol}(\partial W_{H},\alpha_{H})=\frac{1}{n!}\int_{W_{H}}(d\hat{\lambda})^{n+1}=(n+1)\operatorname{Vol}(W_{H}). Using the identityVol⁡(WH)=1n!​Cal⁡(H)\operatorname{Vol}(W_{H})=\frac{1}{n!}\operatorname{Cal}(H), we obtainVol⁡(∂WH,αH)=n+1n!​Cal⁡(H)\operatorname{Vol}(\partial W_{H},\alpha_{H})=\frac{n+1}{n!}\operatorname{Cal}(H).
Substituting this identity into the two estimates forSys⁡(∂WH)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})givesn!min{Sys(∂W),τmin(H)}n+1(n+1)​Cal⁡(H)≤ρ​(WH)≤n!Sys(∂W)n+1(n+1)​Cal⁡(H),\frac{n!\,\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\min}(H)\}^{n+1}}{(n+1)\operatorname{Cal}(H)}\leq\rho(W_{H})\leq\frac{n!\,\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W)^{n+1}}{(n+1)\operatorname{Cal}(H)},

as requested.
∎

Setτmax​(H)≔maxS1×W⁡τλ,H\tau_{\max}(H)\coloneqq\max_{S^{1}\times W}\tau_{\lambda,H}, then a similar argument as in Proposition1.31yields the following result.

## Corollary 1.32.

Let(W2​n,λ)(W^{2n},\lambda)be a Liouville domain withχ​(W)≠0\chi(W)\neq 0. Then for anyH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)we haveSys⁡(∂WH)≤min⁡{Sys⁡(∂W),τmax​(H)}\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\leq\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\max}(H)\}. Consequently,n!min{Sys(∂W),τmin(H)}n+1(n+1)​Cal⁡(H)≤ρ​(WH)≤n!min{Sys(∂W),τmax(H)}n+1(n+1)​Cal⁡(H).\frac{n!\,\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\min}(H)\}^{n+1}}{(n+1)\operatorname{Cal}(H)}\leq\rho(W_{H})\leq\frac{n!\,\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\max}(H)\}^{n+1}}{(n+1)\operatorname{Cal}(H)}.

## Proof.

Since the time-one mapφH1:W→W\varphi_{H}^{1}\colon W\to Wis Hamiltonian isotopic, and hence homotopic, to the identity, its Lefschetz number satisfiesL​(φH1)=L​(IdW)=χ​(W)≠0L(\varphi_{H}^{1})=L(\operatorname{Id}_{W})=\chi(W)\neq 0. By the Lefschetz fixed point theorem,φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}has a fixed pointw0∈Ww_{0}\in W; see[21, Theorem 2C.3].

Ifw0∈Int​(W)w_{0}\in{\rm Int}(W), then Corollary2.10, applied withk=1k=1, gives a closed Reeb orbit on∂WH\partial W_{H}of periodTt0,w0,1=∫01τλ,H​(σ,φHσ​(t0,w0))​𝑑σ≤τmax​(H)T_{t_{0},w_{0},1}=\int_{0}^{1}\tau_{\lambda,H}\bigl(\sigma,\varphi_{H}^{\sigma}(t_{0},w_{0})\bigr)\,d\sigma\leq\tau_{\max}(H). So,Sys⁡(∂WH)≤τmax​(H)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\leq\tau_{\max}(H).

Ifw0∈∂Ww_{0}\in\partial W, then, sinceHHis autonomous near∂W\partial WandH|∂W≡0H|_{\partial W}\equiv 0, the Hamiltonian vector field restricts to a reparametrization of the Reeb vector field of(∂W,λ|∂W)(\partial W,\lambda|_{\partial W}). Thus the fixed point ofφH1\varphi_{H}^{1}gives a closed Reeb orbit on the binding∂W×{0}⊂∂WH\partial W\times\{0\}\subset\partial W_{H}with period bounded above byτmax​(H)\tau_{\max}(H). Again,Sys⁡(∂WH)≤τmax​(H)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\leq\tau_{\max}(H). On the other hand, the binding∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}carries the original Reeb flow, soSys⁡(∂WH)≤Sys⁡(∂W)\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\leq\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W). ThereforeSys⁡(∂WH)≤min⁡{Sys⁡(∂W),τmax​(H)}\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W_{H})\leq\min\{\operatorname{Sys}(\partial W),\tau_{\max}(H)\}.
∎

The upper bound in Proposition1.31is not particularly useful, since wheneverCal⁡(H)→∞\operatorname{Cal}(H)\to\infty, we obtainρ​(WH)→0\rho(W_{H})\to 0, which is essentially obvious. The lower bound in the same proposition is more useful: if a sequence of Hamiltonians{Hn}n∈ℕ⊂ℋ​(W,λ)\{H_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)satisfiesminS1×W⁡τλ,Hn≥κ\min_{S^{1}\times W}\tau_{\lambda,H_{n}}\geq\kappafor some positiveκ\kappaindependent ofnn, whileCal⁡(Hn)→0\operatorname{Cal}(H_{n})\to 0, then we obtain a sequence of Liouville domains{WHn}n∈ℕ\{W_{H_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}withρ​(WHn)→∞\rho(W_{H_{n}})\to\infty. Note that, unlike the Anosov–Katok conjugation construction in[2], where the input Hamiltonians may take negative values, our input HamiltoniansHHare by definition always non-negative; hence the volume conditionCal⁡(H)→0\operatorname{Cal}(H)\to 0is in fact a rather strong constraint. This makes it more difficult to construct a sequence of Hamiltonians{Hn}n∈ℕ\{H_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}yielding divergent{ρ​(WHn)}n∈ℕ\{\rho(W_{H_{n}})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}. For instance, when(W,λstd)⊂(ℂn,λstd)(W,\lambda_{\rm std})\subset(\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda_{\rm std})is a Liouville domain inℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}, it seems impossible to construct such a sequence using only radial Hamiltonian functionsHH, i.e.,H​(t,z)=H​(π​|z|2)H(t,z)=H(\pi|z|^{2}), for whichτλstd,H\tau_{\lambda_{\rm std},H}admits a clear geometric interpretation (see the proof of TheoremG). In fact, for radial input Hamiltonians, the conditionCal⁡(Hn)→0\operatorname{Cal}(H_{n})\to 0implies thatminS1×W⁡τλ,Hn→0\min_{S^{1}\times W}\tau_{\lambda,H_{n}}\to 0asn→∞n\to\infty.

## Remark 1.33.

From the proof of Proposition1.31, one can sharpen the lower bound in the resulting estimate by replacingτmin\tau_{\rm min}bymin⁡Tt0,w0,k\min T_{t_{0},w_{0},k}, i.e., the minimal period of integer-periodic Hamiltonian orbits ofHH. Still, it is a nontrivial task to controlmin⁡Tt0,w0,k\min T_{t_{0},w_{0},k}so that it remains relatively large (even asCal⁡(H)\operatorname{Cal}(H)approaches0).

## 1.8.Categorification

Generalizing the tube construction above, given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), consider two input Hamiltonian functionsH−,H+∈ℋ​(W,λ)H_{-},H_{+}\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)—that is, satisfying condition (3)—and further required to satisfyH−≤H+H_{-}\leq H_{+}pointwise. One can then consider(17)WH−,H+≔{(w,z)∈W×ℂ|z=r​e2​π​i​t​and​H−​(t,w)≤π​|z|2≤H+​(t,w)}.W_{H_{-},H_{+}}\coloneqq\left\{(w,z)\in W\times\mathbb{C}\,|\,z=re^{2\pi it}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,H_{-}(t,w)\leq\pi|z|^{2}\leq H_{+}(t,w)\right\}.

Suppose in addition thatH−​(t,w)<H+​(t,w)H_{-}(t,w)<H_{+}(t,w)for(t,w)∈S1×Int​(W)(t,w)\in S^{1}\times{\rm Int}(W). ThenWH−,H+W_{H_{-},H_{+}}is a manifold with corners that can be expressed asWH−,H+=WH+\WH−¯W_{H_{-},H_{+}}=\overline{W_{H_{+}}\backslash W_{H_{-}}}, where∂WH−,H+=∂WH−∪∂W×{0}∂WH+.\partial W_{H_{-},H_{+}}=\partial W_{H_{-}}\cup_{\partial W\times\{0\}}\partial W_{H_{+}}.

We callWH−,H+W_{H_{-},H_{+}}atunnel construction. WhenH−=0H_{-}=0(even though0∉ℋ​(W,λ)0\notin\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)), the tunnel construction reduces to the tube constructionWH+W_{H_{+}}in (2). In the other extreme caseH−=H+(=H)H_{-}=H_{+}(=H), the tunnelWH−,H+W_{H_{-},H_{+}}reduces to the contact manifold(∂WH,λ^|∂WH)(\partial W_{H},\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}).

This picture is useful for keeping track of the fiberwise nature of the construction. For each base pointw∈Ww\in W, the fiber(WH1,H2)w(W_{H_{1},H_{2}})_{w}is the annular region in theℂ\mathbb{C}-coordinate cut out by the two radii determined byH1H_{1}andH2H_{2}; over the boundary∂W\partial W, this annular fiber collapses to the common corner∂W×{0}\partial W\times\{0\}. Thus the tunnel should be viewed as a family of annuli over the baseWW, interpolating between the lower and upper tube boundaries∂WH1\partial W_{H_{1}}and∂WH2\partial W_{H_{2}}.Figure 2.A schematic picture of the tunnel constructionWH1,H2W_{H_{1},H_{2}}

The tunnel construction provides a geometric realization of the Thompson-type metric introduced in Definition1.3. To elaborate, we first introduce the following quantity associated to a tunnel construction.

## Definition 1.34.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain. ForH−,H+∈ℋ​(W,λ)H_{-},H_{+}\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)withH−≤H+H_{-}\leq H_{+}, thethicknessof the tunnelWH−,H+W_{H_{-},H_{+}}defined in (17) is given by(18)ℓ​(WH−,H+)≔ln⁡(sup(t,w)∈S1×Int​(W)H+​(t,w)H−​(t,w))∈[0,∞].\ell(W_{H_{-},H_{+}})\coloneqq\ln\left(\sup_{(t,w)\in S^{1}\times{\rm Int}(W)}\frac{H_{+}(t,w)}{H_{-}(t,w)}\right)\in[0,\infty].

In the degenerate caseH−=H+H_{-}=H_{+}(whereWH−,H+W_{H_{-},H_{+}}is a contact manifold), setℓ​(WH−,H+)=0\ell(W_{H_{-},H_{+}})=0.

The following lemma shows how the Thompson-type metricdTd_{\rm T}can be identified with the thickness of tunnels.

## Lemma 1.35.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain. For anyH,G∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), the Thompson-type metricdT​(⋅,⋅)d_{\rm T}(\cdot,\cdot)from Definition1.3and the thicknessℓ​(W⋅,⋅)\ell(W_{\cdot,\cdot})of a tunnel from Definition1.34satisfy the following relation:(19)dT(H,G)=inf{ℓ(WH−,H+)|H−≤min⁡{H,G∘ψ}≤max⁡{H,G∘ψ}≤H+,H−,H+∈ℋ​(W,λ),ψ∈Symp​(W,λ)}.d_{\rm T}(H,G)=\inf\left\{\ell(W_{H_{-},H_{+}})\,\bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{l}H_{-}\leq\min\{H,G\circ\psi\}\leq\max\{H,G\circ\psi\}\leq H_{+},\\
H_{-},H_{+}\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda),\ \psi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)\end{array}\right\}.

Here,(G∘ψ)​(t,w)≔G​(t,ψ​(w))(G\circ\psi)(t,w)\coloneqq G(t,\psi(w)). In other words,dT​(H,G)d_{\rm T}(H,G)measures the smallest thickness of a tunnel containing, up to symplectomorphism, both∂WH\partial W_{H}and∂WG\partial W_{G}as interior level sets.

## Remark 1.36.

When the input Hamiltonian functionsH,G∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)already satisfyH≤GH\leq G, takingψ=id\psi={\rm id}in Lemma1.35yieldsdT​(H,G)≤ℓ​(WH,G)d_{\rm T}(H,G)\leq\ell(W_{H,G}), whereWH,GW_{H,G}is well-defined.

Geometrically, the tunnel construction admits acompositionin the following sense: ifH1≤H2≤H3∈ℋ​(W,λ)H_{1}\leq H_{2}\leq H_{3}\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), thenWH1,H3≃WH1,H2∪∂WH2WH2,H3,W_{H_{1},H_{3}}\simeq W_{H_{1},H_{2}}\cup_{\partial W_{H_{2}}}W_{H_{2},H_{3}},

where the gluing is carried out trivially along the contact manifold∂WH2\partial W_{H_{2}}, and “≃\simeq” denotes isomorphism as Liouville cobordisms. Meanwhile, it is readily verified that the thickness is subadditive:ℓ​(WH1,H3)≤ℓ​(WH1,H2)+ℓ​(WH2,H3)\ell(W_{H_{1},H_{3}})\leq\ell(W_{H_{1},H_{2}})+\ell(W_{H_{2},H_{3}}). This suggests that the Thompson-type metricdTd_{\rm T}can be formulated categorically, with quantitative measurement given by the thickness function. In fact, such a “metric” category was introduced—as a categorification of a metric space—by Lawvere in[27]. In short, aLawvere metric categoryis a category333In general, anormed categorymeans associating a norm to every hom-set of the category. Here, a Lawvere metric category is simply a normed category in which every hom-set contains exactly one element.enriched in the monoidal poset([0,∞],≥,+,0)([0,\infty],\geq,+,0). The following result, one of the main results of this paper, explicitly lists the axioms of such a category.

## Theorem H.

Let(W,λ)(W,\lambda)be a Liouville domain. Then the function spaceℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)from (6) can be equipped with a Lawvere metric category structure, denoted byTun​(W,λ){\rm Tun}(W,\lambda). Explicitly,Ob​(Tun​(W,λ))≔ℋ​(W,λ){\rm Ob}({\rm Tun}(W,\lambda))\coloneqq\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), and forH,G∈Ob​(Tun​(W,λ))H,G\in{\rm Ob}({\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)),(20)homTun​(W,λ)​(H,G)≔infψ∈Symp​(W,λ)max⁡{ln⁡(sup(t,w)∈S1×Int​(W)H​(t,w)G​(t,ψ​(w))),0}.{\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}(H,G)\coloneqq\inf_{\psi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)}\max\left\{\ln\left(\sup_{(t,w)\in S^{1}\times{\rm Int}(W)}\frac{H(t,w)}{G(t,\psi(w))}\right),0\right\}.

Then the following two properties hold:
- (i)

homTun​(W,λ)​(H,H)=0{\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}(H,H)=0for anyH∈Ob​(Tun​(W,λ))H\in{\rm Ob}({\rm Tun}(W,\lambda));
- (ii)

homTun​(W,λ)​(H1,H3)≤homTun​(W,λ)​(H1,H2)+homTun​(W,λ)​(H2,H3){\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}(H_{1},H_{3})\leq{\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}(H_{1},H_{2})+{\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}(H_{2},H_{3})for any objectsH1,H2,H3∈Ob​(Tun​(W,λ))H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}\in{\rm Ob}({\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)).

Moreover, one can symmetrizehomTun​(W,λ){\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}by defininghomTun​(W,λ)sym​(H,G)≔infψ∈Symp​(W,λ)ln⁡(sup(t,w)∈S1×Int​(W)max⁡{H​(t,w)G​(t,ψ​(w)),G​(t,ψ​(w))H​(t,w)}).{\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}^{\rm sym}(H,G)\coloneqq\inf_{\psi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)}\ln\left(\sup_{(t,w)\in S^{1}\times{\rm Int}(W)}\max\left\{\frac{H(t,w)}{G(t,\psi(w))},\frac{G(t,\psi(w))}{H(t,w)}\right\}\right).

ThenhomTun​(W,λ)sym​(H,G)=dT​(H,G){\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}^{\rm sym}(H,G)=d_{\rm T}(H,G), the Thompson-type metric in Definition1.3.

By Lemma1.35and the last conclusion of TheoremH, the symmetrizationhomTun​(W,λ)sym{\rm hom}_{{\rm Tun}(W,\lambda)}^{\rm sym}in TheoremHadmits a geometric interpretation in terms of the tunnel construction in (17) and the thickness function in (18).

## Remark 1.37.

In a similar fashion, one can formulate the pseudo-metric spaces discussed above,(𝒟2​n,dSBM)(\mathscr{D}_{2n},d_{\rm SBM})and({contact 1-forms},dCBM)(\{\mbox{contact 1-forms}\},d_{\rm CBM}), as Lawvere metric categories. TheoremsAandBthen show that the mapsH∈ℋ+​(W,λ)↦U​(H)H\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)\mapsto U(H)andH∈ℋ+​(W,λ)↦αHH\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)\mapsto\alpha_{H}are “1-Lipschitz” functors with respect to these Lawvere metric structures. We leave the verification of the details to the interested reader.

## 1.9.Floer homology of the tube

The tube construction yields a Liouville domainWHW_{H}. Recall that its Liouville completionWH^\widehat{W_{H}}, obtained by gluing a cylindrical end[1,∞)×∂WH[1,\infty)\times\partial W_{H}toWHW_{H}, also admits a Floer theory called symplectic homology, with an equivariant version denoted bySH∗⁡(WH)\operatorname{SH}_{*}(W_{H}). By definition, the generators ofSH∗⁡(WH)\operatorname{SH}_{*}(W_{H})can be identified with the closed Reeb orbits on∂WH\partial W_{H}. Slowing down the picture, one can viewSH∗​(WH){\rm SH}_{*}(W_{H})as a limit of Hamiltonian Floer homologies with respect to Viterbo-type (admissible) functionsKNK_{N}, whereKNK_{N}isC2C^{2}-small insideint​(WH){\rm int}(W_{H})and linear with admissible slopeNNon a sufficiently large end of[1,∞)×∂WH[1,\infty)\times\partial W_{H}. As usual, this Floer homology is denoted byHF∗​(KN){\rm HF}_{*}(K_{N}). By Corollary2.10, the generators of the corresponding chain complexCF∗​(KN,J){\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N},J)(well-defined for a generic choice of almost complex structureJJ) can be classified into the following three subclasses:(21)𝒫​(KN)=𝒫Ncst⊔𝒫ND⊔∐1≤k≤κ​(N)𝒫N(k).\mathcal{P}(K_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\mathrm{cst}}\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{N}^{D}\sqcup\coprod_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}.

Hereκ​(N)\kappa(N)denotes the maximal winding number that can occur among the Hamiltonian orbits ofKNK_{N}with admissible slopeNN,DDdenotesW×{0}W\times\{0\}, a divisor (with boundary) inWHW_{H}, and for an orbitγ\gammanot contained inDD, itswinding numberis set to bek​(γ)≔(2​π)−1​∫γ𝑑θℂk(\gamma)\coloneqq(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\gamma}d\theta_{\mathbb{C}}; that is, the rotation number around thezz-axis in theℂ\mathbb{C}-component of the tube construction. In (21),𝒫Ncst\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\mathrm{cst}}denotes the off-divisor constant orbits in a compact region,𝒫ND\mathcal{P}_{N}^{D}denotes the orbits contained inDD, and𝒫N(k)\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}denotes the integer-periodic Hamiltonian orbits (of the Hamiltonian flow generated byHH) with winding numberk≥1k\geq 1.

We declare the constant orbits in𝒫Ncst\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\mathrm{cst}}and the divisor orbits in𝒫ND\mathcal{P}_{N}^{D}to have winding number zero. Then, as𝕂\mathbb{K}-modules, we have the following decomposition:(22)CF∗(KN,J)=𝕂⟨𝒫Ncst⊔𝒫ND⟩⊕⨁1≤k≤κ​(N)𝕂⟨𝒫N(k)⟩=:CN,∗(0)⊕⨁1≤k≤κ​(N)CN,∗(k).{\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N},J)=\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\mathrm{cst}}\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{N}^{D}\rangle\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}\rangle=:C_{N,*}^{(0)}\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}C_{N,*}^{(k)}.

We call the Floer datum(KN,J)(K_{N},J)DD-compatibleifXKN|D⊂T​DX_{K_{N}}|_{D}\subset TDandJ​T​D=T​DJTD=TD, andregularif the moduli spaces used to define the differential are cut out transversely. A local argument, due to the dimension reason fromDD, shows that such Floer data is generic.

Upgrading to a chain complex, for any two such generatorsγ−,γ+\gamma_{-},\,\gamma_{+}in𝒫​(KN)\mathcal{P}(K_{N}), one investigates the standard Floer cylinders fromγ−\gamma_{-}toγ+\gamma_{+}inWH^\widehat{W_{H}}perturbed by the Hamiltonian vector field ofKNK_{N}. The following lemma provides a topological constraint on these Floer cylinders.

## Lemma 1.38.

Letu:ℝ×S1→WH^u\colon\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\widehat{W_{H}}be a finite-energy Floer cylinder for a regularDD-compatible datum(KN,J)(K_{N},J), with asymptoticslims→−∞u​(s,⋅)=γ−\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,\cdot)=\gamma_{-}andlims→+∞u​(s,⋅)=γ+\lim_{s\to+\infty}u(s,\cdot)=\gamma_{+}in𝒫​(KN)\mathcal{P}(K_{N}). Suppose that neither asymptotic orbit is contained inDD. Thenu⋅D=k​(γ+)−k​(γ−)≥0.u\cdot D=k(\gamma_{+})-k(\gamma_{-})\geq 0.

Moreover,u⋅D=0u\cdot D=0if and only ifu∩D=∅u\cap D=\varnothing. In particular, when restricted to Floer cylinders connecting orbits in∐1≤k≤κ​(N)𝒫N(k)\coprod_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}, the Floer differential∂KN,J\partial_{K_{N},J}for the Floer chain complexCF∗​(KN,J){\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N},J)cannotdecrease the winding number.

The relation in Lemma1.38is illustrated schematically in Figure3. The left-hand panel depicts a Floer cylinder disjoint from the divisor; in this case the intersection number vanishes and the two asymptotic orbits have the same winding number. The right-hand panel depicts a cylinder with positive intersections withDD; by positivity of intersections, each crossing contributes+1+1, and the total contribution is exactly the increase of the winding number fromγ−\gamma_{-}toγ+\gamma_{+}.Figure 3.Floer cylinders relative to the divisorDD

Importantly, note that Lemma1.38doesnotexclude the possibility that a Floer cylinder connecting orbits in∐1≤k≤κ​(N)𝒫N(k)\coprod_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}may intersectDD. Therefore, without invoking further constraints, we can at present only conclude that, with respect to the decomposition in (22), the differential∂KN,J\partial_{K_{N},J}is merely lower triangular:(23)∂KN,J|⨁1≤k≤κ​(N)CN,∗(k)=(∂N(1)00⋯0∗∂N(2)0⋯0∗∗⋱⋱⋮⋮⋮⋱∂N(κ​(N)−1)0∗∗⋯∗∂N(κ​(N))).\partial_{K_{N},J}\big|_{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}C_{N,*}^{(k)}}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{N}^{(1)}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\ast&\partial_{N}^{(2)}&0&\cdots&0\\
\ast&\ast&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\partial_{N}^{(\kappa(N)-1)}&0\\
\ast&\ast&\cdots&\ast&\partial_{N}^{(\kappa(N))}\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, the entries “∗\ast” in the matrix (23) precisely capture those Floer cylinders connectingγ−∈𝒫N(k)\gamma_{-}\in\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}andγ+∈𝒫N(ℓ)\gamma_{+}\in\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(\ell)}with different winding numbersk≠ℓk\neq\ell(so that these cylinders must pass through the divisorDD).

Back toCF∗​(KN,J){\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N},J)in (22), let us denoteCF∗​(K∞,J):\displaystyle{\rm CF}_{*}(K_{\infty},J):=lim→N∈ℕ⁡CF∗​(KN,J)\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{N\in\mathbb{N}}{\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N},J)=lim→N∈ℕ⁡(𝕂​⟨𝒫Ncst⊔𝒫ND⟩⊕⨁1≤k≤κ​(N)𝕂​⟨𝒫N(k)⟩)\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\mathrm{cst}}\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{N}^{D}\rangle\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}\rangle\right)=(lim→N∈ℕ⁡𝕂​⟨𝒫Ncst⟩)⏟cst-orbits⊕(lim→N∈ℕ⁡𝕂​⟨𝒫ND⟩)⏟D-orbits⊕(lim→N∈ℕ⁡⨁1≤k≤κ​(N)𝕂​⟨𝒫N(k)⟩)⏟Ham-orbits\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\varinjlim_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\mathrm{cst}}\rangle\right)}_{\text{cst-orbits}}\oplus\underbrace{\left(\varinjlim_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{D}\rangle\right)}_{\text{$D$-orbits}}\oplus\underbrace{\left(\varinjlim_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq\kappa(N)}\mathbb{K}\langle\mathcal{P}_{N}^{(k)}\rangle\right)}_{\text{Ham-orbits}}

where the direct limit in the first line is taken over inclusionsCF∗​(KN,J)→CF∗​(KN+1,J){\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N},J)\to{\rm CF}_{*}(K_{N+1},J). To simplify the setting, by working on strictly positive filtration levels, one can ignore the off-divisor constant orbits, the part labelled by “cst-orbits”. Then the remaining generators consist of all the closed Reeb orbits on the contact boundary∂W\partial W(which come from the part labelled by “DD-orbits”) and all the integer-periodic Hamiltonian orbits ofHHin the interior ofWW(which come from the part labelled by “Ham-orbits”).

Again, when restricting on the Hamiltonian orbits part, the differential, now naturally denoted by∂K∞,J|⟨Ham-orbits⟩\partial_{K_{\infty},J}|_{\tiny{\left<\mbox{Ham-orbits}\right>}}, is in the same form as in (23). However, the following Example1.39shows that Reeb orbits in divisorDDand Hamiltonian orbits must interacted. In other words,∂K∞,J\partial_{K_{\infty},J}will mix different types of Reeb orbits on the contact boundary∂WH\partial W_{H}, and the classification via winding numbers as in the defining equation in (22) doesnotnecessarily split the differential∂K∞,J\partial_{K_{\infty},J}.

## Example 1.39.

In this example, we revisit the standard four-dimensional ellipsoidE​(1,a)E(1,a)from the perspective of the tube construction in (2). We take the standard two-dimensional disk(B¯2​(1),λstd)⊂(ℂ,λstd)(\overline{B}^{2}(1),\lambda_{\rm std})\subset(\mathbb{C},\lambda_{\rm std})as the initial Liouville domain and denote it by(W,λ)(W,\lambda)for notational consistency. Recall that the symbol ‘11’ inB¯2​(1)\overline{B}^{2}(1)indicates that its standard Euclidean area is equal to11(see the convention at the beginning of Section1.5). Fix an irrational scalara>1a>1, and consider the following radially symmetric Hamiltonian function onB¯2​(1)\overline{B}^{2}(1), written in coordinatesw=(r,θ)w=(r,\theta):H​(r,θ)=a​(1−π​r2)=a​(1−π​|w|2),r∈[0,1].H(r,\theta)=a(1-\pi r^{2})=a(1-\pi|w|^{2}),\qquad r\in[0,1].

By (2), we obtainWH={(w,z)|π​|z|2≤a​(1−π​|w|2)}W_{H}=\{(w,z)\,|\,\pi|z|^{2}\leq a(1-\pi|w|^{2})\}, which is preciselyE​(1,a)E(1,a).

For the generators ofCF∗​(K∞,J)\mathrm{CF}_{*}(K_{\infty},J), apart from the constant ones, we have the following:
- (i)

DD-orbits: iterates of the full11-rotation along the boundary∂B¯2​(1)\partial\overline{B}^{2}(1), denoted byγDk\gamma_{D}^{k}fork∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}, with periods𝒜​(γDk)=k\mathcal{A}(\gamma_{D}^{k})=k.
- (ii)

Ham-orbits: iterates of the simple orbit arising from the unique critical point ofH​(r,θ)H(r,\theta)at the origin ofB¯2​(1)\overline{B}^{2}(1), denoted byγHℓ\gamma_{H}^{\ell}forℓ∈ℕ\ell\in\mathbb{N}, with periods𝒜​(γHℓ)=ℓ​a\mathcal{A}(\gamma_{H}^{\ell})=\ell a.

It is standard that their Conley–Zehnder indices are given by(24)CZ​(γDk)=2​k+1+2​⌊k/a⌋,CZ​(γHℓ)=2​ℓ+1+2​⌊ℓ​a⌋.\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{D}^{k})=2k+1+2\lfloor k/a\rfloor,\qquad\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{H}^{\ell})=2\ell+1+2\lfloor\ell a\rfloor.

These formulas can be recovered in the tube setting as in Proposition 4.3 and Proposition 4.5 of[40]. Meanwhile, the winding number ofγHℓ\gamma_{H}^{\ell}is equal toℓ\ell, hence in particular non-zero. Therefore, Lemma1.38is trivial in this case, since the winding number grows proportionally to the action of the orbitsγHℓ\gamma_{H}^{\ell}, plus that the positive energy of any connecting Floer cylinder is non-negative.

Moreover, the restriction of the differential∂K∞,J\partial_{K_{\infty},J}(counting only index 1 Floer cylinders) to the subspace generated by the Ham-orbits satisfies∂K∞,J|⟨Ham​-​orbits⟩=0\partial_{K_{\infty},J}|_{\left<\mathrm{Ham\text{-}orbits}\right>}=0

since the collection of Ham-orbits consists precisely of theγHℓ\gamma_{H}^{\ell}, and by the index formula (24) above, the Conley–Zehnder indices of any two distinct such orbits differ by at least22. Consequently, if the Ham-orbits alone formed a chain complex, it would be simply an infinite direct sum of copies of the ground field𝕂\mathbb{K}. On the other hand, it is a standard fact that the positive symplectic homology ofE​(1,a)E(1,a)is one-dimensional, i.e.SH+​(E​(1,a))≅𝕂{\rm SH}_{+}(E(1,a))\cong\mathbb{K}. This forces infinitely many homological cancellations to occur between theγHℓ\gamma_{H}^{\ell}and theγDk\gamma_{D}^{k}.

Notwithstanding the complexity of the differential∂K∞,J\partial_{K_{\infty},J}, one may pursue the following two approaches. First, one can reduce the orbit pool in the construction of (positive) symplectic homology by imposing homotopy constraints (see either[35]or[38]), since Floer cylinders preserve the homotopy type of orbits. This requires that the initial Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda)possess sufficient topological complexity, e.g., a high-genus surface with disks removed. Second, one can investigate more deeply the mixed-type Floer differential relations betweenDD-orbits and Ham-orbits via symplectic field theory (SFT). More concretely, one may consider a neighborhood of the divisorDDinsideWHW_{H}, perform neck-stretching for a Floer cylinder, and seek quantitative restrictions arising from SFT-compactness (cf.[14]in the setting of closed ambient symplectic manifolds). These directions will be investigated in detail in the future work[16].

## 2.Background

In this section, we briefly recall the dynamics on Liouville domains and study the properties of the domain(WH,λ^)(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})constructed in (2). In addition, we establish a few preparatory propositions that will be used in the proofs of our main theorems.

## 2.1.Tube construction revisited

ALiouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is a compact exact symplectic manifold with boundary, where the Liouville vector fieldYλY_{\lambda}, defined byιYλ​d​λ=λ\iota_{Y_{\lambda}}d\lambda=\lambda, points outwards along∂W\partial W. Therefore for allt<0t<0the time-ttLiouville flowℒλt:W→W\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{t}\colon W\rightarrow Wis well-defined. Theskeletonof(W,λ)(W,\lambda)is defined bySk​(W)≔⋂t>0ℒλ−t​(W).{\rm Sk}(W)\coloneqq\bigcap_{t>0}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{-t}(W).

Given a compactly supported time-dependent HamiltonianH:S1×W2​n→ℝH\colon S^{1}\times W^{2n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}, letφHt\varphi_{H}^{t}denote its Hamiltonian flow, obtained by integrating the Hamiltonian vector fieldXHtX_{H_{t}}determined byιXHt​d​λ=dw​Ht\iota_{X_{H_{t}}}d\lambda=d_{w}H_{t}. Forψ≔φH1\psi\coloneqq\varphi_{H}^{1}, the Calabi invariant is defined byCal⁡(ψ)≔∫S1×WH​𝑑t∧(d​λ)n\operatorname{Cal}(\psi)\coloneqq\int_{S^{1}\times W}Hdt\wedge(d\lambda)^{n}

By Lemma 10.3.4 in[30], the above integral is independent of the choice of compactly supported HamiltonianHHgeneratingψ\psi. A curveγ:[0,1]→W\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow Wis called a11-periodic orbit ofHHifγ˙​(t)=XHt​(γ​(t))\dot{\gamma}(t)=X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))andγ​(0)=γ​(1)\gamma(0)=\gamma(1). Its action is given by𝒜H​(γ)≔∫γλ+∫01H​(t,γ​(t))​𝑑t.\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma)\coloneqq\int_{\gamma}\lambda+\int_{0}^{1}H(t,\gamma(t))dt.

The restrictionα≔λ|∂W\alpha\coloneqq\lambda|_{\partial W}defines a contact form on∂W\partial W. Recall that the Reeb vector fieldRαR_{\alpha}of the contact manifold(∂W,ker⁡(α))(\partial W,\ker(\alpha))is uniquely determined byd​α​(Rα,⋅)=0d\alpha(R_{\alpha},\cdot)=0andα​(Rα)=1\alpha(R_{\alpha})=1. Aclosed Reeb orbitin(∂W,ker⁡(α))(\partial W,\ker(\alpha))is a curveγ:[0,T]→∂W\gamma\colon[0,T]\rightarrow\partial Wsatisfyingγ˙​(t)=Rα​(γ​(t))\dot{\gamma}(t)=R_{\alpha}(\gamma(t))andγ​(0)=γ​(T)\gamma(0)=\gamma(T).

Given a Liouville domain(W,λ)(W,\lambda), recall from (6) thatℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)denotes the set of all Hamiltonian functionsH:S1×W→ℝH\colon S^{1}\times W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}satisfying condition (3). Also, recall that for anyH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)we associate a domainWH⊂W×ℂW_{H}\subset W\times\mathbb{C}constructed in (2), equipped with11-formλ^(=λ+π​r2​d​t)∈Ω1​(W×ℂ)\hat{\lambda}(=\lambda+\pi r^{2}dt)\in\Omega^{1}(W\times\mathbb{C}). The following propositions are inspired by Proposition 2.1 in[1]and Proposition 4.1 in[40].

## Proposition 2.1.

For anyH∈ℋ​(W2​n,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W^{2n},\lambda), the domain(WH,λ^)(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})is a Liouville domain with the volumeVol​(WH)=1n!​Cal⁡(H).{\rm Vol}(W_{H})=\frac{1}{n!}\,\operatorname{Cal}(H).

## Proof.

It is immediate thatd​λ^=d​λ+d​x∧d​yd\hat{\lambda}=d\lambda+dx\wedge dyis symplectic onW×ℂW\times\mathbb{C}, hence also onWHW_{H}. Denote byYλY_{\lambda}the Liouville vector field of(W,λ)(W,\lambda), so thatιYλ​d​λ=λ\iota_{Y_{\lambda}}d\lambda=\lambda. Then the Liouville vector field of(W×ℂ,λ^)(W\times\mathbb{C},\hat{\lambda})isY^=Yλ+12​(x​∂x+y​∂y),\widehat{Y}=Y_{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}(x\partial_{x}+y\partial_{y}),sinceιY^​d​λ^=λ^\iota_{\widehat{Y}}d\hat{\lambda}=\hat{\lambda}.

OnW×(ℂ∖{0})W\times(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}), the angular coordinatet=θ/2​π∈S1t=\theta/2\pi\in S^{1}is well-defined and smooth, so we may define a smooth functionfH​(w,z)=π​|z|2−H​(t,w)f_{H}(w,z)=\pi|z|^{2}-H(t,w). ThenWH∩(W×(ℂ∖{0}))=fH−1​((−∞,0]).W_{H}\cap(W\times(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}))=f_{H}^{-1}((-\infty,0]).

SinceHHis autonomous in a neighborhood of∂W\partial W, there exists an open neighborhoodU⊂WU\subset Wof∂W\partial Wand a smooth functionh:U→ℝh\colon U\to\mathbb{R}such thatH​(t,w)=h​(w)H(t,w)=h(w)for all(t,w)∈S1×U(t,w)\in S^{1}\times U. ThengHg_{H}is smooth on all ofU×ℂU\times\mathbb{C}, and by definition,WH∩(U×ℂ)=gH−1​((−∞,0])W_{H}\cap(U\times\mathbb{C})=g_{H}^{-1}((-\infty,0]). ## Corollary 2.2.

For anyH,G∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), we have1n+1​|ln⁡Cal⁡(H)−ln⁡Cal⁡(G)|≤dBM​((WH,λ^),(WG,λ^))≤dSBM​((WH,λ^),(WG,λ^)).\frac{1}{n+1}|\ln\operatorname{Cal}(H)-\ln\operatorname{Cal}(G)|\leq d_{\rm BM}((W_{H},\hat{\lambda}),(W_{G},\hat{\lambda}))\leq d_{\rm SBM}((W_{H},\hat{\lambda}),(W_{G},\hat{\lambda})).

Let(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W})and(V,λV)(V,\lambda_{V})be two2​n2n-dimensional Liouville domains. The following proposition provides a sufficient condition where two tubes, constructed from different input Hamiltonian functions, can be related by a Liouville diffeomorphism.

## Proposition 2.4.

Let{Hs}s∈[0,1]⊂ℋ​(W,λ)\{H^{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)be a smooth family. Assume that the time-one mapφHs1\varphi_{H^{s}}^{1}is independent ofss, and that there exists a neighborhoodUUof∂W\partial Wsuch thatHs=H0​on​S1×UH^{s}=H^{0}\text{ on }S^{1}\times U, for alls∈[0,1]s\in[0,1]. Then there exists a Hamiltonian diffeomorphismψ:W×ℂ→W×ℂ\psi\colon W\times\mathbb{C}\to W\times\mathbb{C}such thatψ​(WH0)=WH1\psi(W_{H^{0}})=W_{H^{1}}. In particular, the restrictionψ|WH0:(WH0,λ^)→(WH1,λ^)\psi|_{W_{H^{0}}}\colon(W_{H^{0}},\hat{\lambda})\to(W_{H^{1}},\hat{\lambda})is a Liouville diffeomorphism.

Since the endpointsϕs0=id\phi_{s}^{0}=\mathrm{id}andϕs1\phi_{s}^{1}are independent ofss, we haveYs0=Ys1=0Y_{s}^{0}=Y_{s}^{1}=0. Moreover, by the argument above,Yst=0Y_{s}^{t}=0onUU. By Banyaga’s Lemma (Proposition 3.1.5 in[5]), there exists a smooth family of HamiltoniansFs:S1×W→ℝF_{s}\colon S^{1}\times W\to\mathbb{R}with support contained inW∖UW\setminus U, such thatYst=XFs​(t,⋅)Y_{s}^{t}=X_{F_{s}(t,\cdot)}and(25)∂sHs=∂tFs−d​Hts​(XFs).\partial_{s}H^{s}=\partial_{t}F_{s}-dH_{t}^{s}(X_{F_{s}}).

This subsection ends with a cute lemma which will be helpful in the proof of TheoremA.

## Lemma 2.5.

Ifψ:(W,λW)→(V,λV)\psi\colon(W,\lambda_{W})\rightarrow(V,\lambda_{V})is a strict Liouville diffeomorphism, then it induces a canonical bijection betweenℋ​(W,λW)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda_{W})andℋ​(V,λV)\mathcal{H}(V,\lambda_{V})viaH↦H∘ψ−1H\mapsto H\circ\psi^{-1}and
a strict Liouville diffeomorphismψH:(WH,λ^W)→(VH∘ψ−1,λ^V)by(w,z)↦(ψ​(w),z).\psi_{H}\colon(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}_{W})\rightarrow(V_{H\circ\psi^{-1}},\hat{\lambda}_{V})\quad\mbox{by}\quad(w,z)\mapsto(\psi(w),z).

In particular,(WH,λ^)≅(WH∘ψ,λ^)(W_{H},\hat{\lambda})\cong(W_{H\circ\psi},\hat{\lambda})for anyψ∈Symp​(W,λ)\psi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda).

## Proof.

LetK≔H∘ψ−1:S1×V→ℝK\coloneqq H\circ\psi^{-1}\colon S^{1}\times V\to\mathbb{R}.
We first show thatK∈ℋ​(V,λV)K\in\mathcal{H}(V,\lambda_{V}). Sinceψ:W→V\psi\colon W\to Vis a diffeomorphism of manifolds with boundary, it maps∂W\partial Wonto∂V\partial V. Hence, becauseHHis autonomous in a neighborhood of∂W\partial Wand satisfiesH|∂W≡0H|_{\partial W}\equiv 0, the same is true forKKin a neighborhood of∂V\partial V. Thus it remains to verify thatτλV,K​(t,v)=λV​(XKt​(v))+K​(t,v)>0.\tau_{\lambda_{V},K}(t,v)=\lambda_{V}(X_{K_{t}}(v))+K(t,v)>0.

To do so, we first identify the Hamiltonian vector field ofKK. Sinceψ\psiis a strict Liouville diffeomorphism, we haveψ∗​λV=λW\psi^{*}\lambda_{V}=\lambda_{W}, henceψ∗​d​λV=d​λW\psi^{*}d\lambda_{V}=d\lambda_{W}. Thereforeψ\psiis a symplectomorphism. For each fixedt∈S1t\in S^{1}, we haveKt=Ht∘ψ−1K_{t}=H_{t}\circ\psi^{-1}. Sinceψ\psiis a strict Liouville diffeomorphism, it is symplectic. Hence,ιψ∗​XHt​d​λV=(ψ−1)∗​(ιXHt​d​λW)=(ψ−1)∗​dw​Ht=dv​Kt.\iota_{\psi_{*}X_{H_{t}}}d\lambda_{V}=(\psi^{-1})^{*}(\iota_{X_{H_{t}}}d\lambda_{W})=(\psi^{-1})^{*}d_{w}H_{t}=d_{v}K_{t}.

ThereforeXKt=ψ∗​XHtX_{K_{t}}=\psi_{*}X_{H_{t}}. Then, usingψ∗​λV=λW\psi^{*}\lambda_{V}=\lambda_{W}, we obtainψ∗​τλV,K​(t,⋅)=ψ∗​(λV​(XKt)+Kt)=λW​(XHt)+Ht=τλW,H​(t,⋅).\psi^{*}\tau_{\lambda_{V},K}(t,\cdot)=\psi^{*}(\lambda_{V}(X_{K_{t}})+K_{t})=\lambda_{W}(X_{H_{t}})+H_{t}=\tau_{\lambda_{W},H}(t,\cdot).SinceτλW,H>0\tau_{\lambda_{W},H}>0by assumption, we conclude thatτλV,K>0\tau_{\lambda_{V},K}>0. ThusK=H∘ψ−1∈ℋ​(V,λV)K=H\circ\psi^{-1}\in\mathcal{H}(V,\lambda_{V}).

Next, defineψH:WH→VH∘ψ−1\psi_{H}\colon W_{H}\to V_{H\circ\psi^{-1}}by(w,z)↦(ψ​(w),z)(w,z)\mapsto(\psi(w),z).
We check that this map is well defined. If(w,z)∈WH(w,z)\in W_{H}, writingz=r​ei​θz=re^{i\theta}withθ=2​π​t\theta=2\pi t, we haveπ​|z|2≤H​(t,w)=(H∘ψ−1)​(t,ψ​(w))\pi|z|^{2}\leq H(t,w)=(H\circ\psi^{-1})(t,\psi(w)), so(ψ​(w),z)∈VH∘ψ−1(\psi(w),z)\in V_{H\circ\psi^{-1}}. Since the inverse map is simply(v,z)↦(ψ−1​(v),z)(v,z)\mapsto(\psi^{-1}(v),z),
the mapψH\psi_{H}is a diffeomorphism.

Finally, we compute the pullback of the Liouville form,ψH∗​λ^V=ψH∗​(λV+12​r2​d​θ)=ψ∗​λV+12​r2​d​θ=λW+12​r2​d​θ=λ^W\psi_{H}^{*}\hat{\lambda}_{V}=\psi_{H}^{*}\left(\lambda_{V}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\,d\theta\right)=\psi^{*}\lambda_{V}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\,d\theta=\lambda_{W}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\,d\theta=\hat{\lambda}_{W}.
ThereforeψH\psi_{H}is a strict Liouville diffeomorphismψH:(WH,λ^W)→(VH∘ψ−1,λ^V)\psi_{H}\colon(W_{H},\hat{\lambda}_{W})\to(V_{H\circ\psi^{-1}},\hat{\lambda}_{V}).

This proves that the assignmentH↦H∘ψ−1H\mapsto H\circ\psi^{-1}gives a bijection fromℋ​(W,λW)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda_{W})toℋ​(V,λV)\mathcal{H}(V,\lambda_{V}), with inverseK↦K∘ψK\mapsto K\circ\psi.
∎

## 2.2.Reeb dynamics on the lift

We now study the dynamics on∂WH\partial W_{H}. WhenHHis autonomous, the following results recover those from Section 4 in[40]. We begin by computing the Reeb vector field.

## Proposition 2.6.

LetH∈ℋ​(W,λ)H\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)and setαH≔λ^|∂WH\alpha_{H}\coloneqq\hat{\lambda}|_{\partial W_{H}}. Then, on∂WH∩{r>0}\partial W_{H}\cap\{r>0\}, the Reeb vector field ofαH\alpha_{H}is given byRαH=1τλ,H​(XHt+∂tH2​π​r​∂r+2​π​∂θ).R_{\alpha_{H}}=\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}}\left(X_{H_{t}}+\frac{\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r}\partial_{r}+2\pi\partial_{\theta}\right).

Equivalently, sinceθ=2​π​t\theta=2\pi t, one may writeRαH=1τλ,H​(XHt+∂tH2​π​r​∂r+∂t).R_{\alpha_{H}}=\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}}\left(X_{H_{t}}+\frac{\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r}\partial_{r}+\partial_{t}\right).

Moreover, this vector field extends smoothly to the entire∂WH\partial W_{H}.

## Proof.

SetF​(w,r,t)≔π​r2−H​(t,w),F(w,r,t)\coloneqq\pi r^{2}-H(t,w),so that∂WH={F=0}\partial W_{H}=\{F=0\}. Sinceθ=2​π​t\theta=2\pi t, we haveλ^=λ+π​r2​d​t\hat{\lambda}=\lambda+\pi r^{2}dtandd​λ^=d​λ+2​π​r​d​r∧d​td\hat{\lambda}=d\lambda+2\pi r\,dr\wedge dt. On∂WH∩{r>0}\partial W_{H}\cap\{r>0\}, write the Reeb vector field in the formR=a​XHt+b​∂r+c​∂tR=aX_{H_{t}}+b\partial_{r}+c\partial_{t}, wherea,b,ca,b,care functions to be determined.

The conditionιR​d​αH=0\iota_{R}d\alpha_{H}=0is equivalent to saying that, along∂WH\partial W_{H}, the11-formιR​d​λ^\iota_{R}d\hat{\lambda}is proportional tod​FdF. Thus there exists a functionggon∂WH\partial W_{H}such thatιR​d​λ^=g​d​F\iota_{R}d\hat{\lambda}=g\,dF. UsingιXHt​d​λ=dw​Ht\iota_{X_{H_{t}}}d\lambda=d_{w}H_{t}, we computeιR​d​λ^=a​dw​Ht+2​π​r​b​d​t−2​π​r​c​d​r\iota_{R}d\hat{\lambda}=a\,d_{w}H_{t}+2\pi r\,b\,dt-2\pi r\,c\,dr. On the other hand,d​F=2​π​r​d​r−dw​Ht−(∂tH)​d​tdF=2\pi r\,dr-d_{w}H_{t}-(\partial_{t}H)\,dt. Comparing the coefficients ofdw​Ht,d​td_{w}H_{t},dtandd​rdr, we obtaina=−g,b=−g​∂tH2​π​r,and​c=−g.a=-g,\,\,b=-\frac{g\,\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r},\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,c=-g.

It remains to impose the normalization conditionαH​(R)=1\alpha_{H}(R)=1. Sinceπ​r2=H​(t,w)\pi r^{2}=H(t,w)on∂WH\partial W_{H}, we have1=λ^​(R)=a​λ​(XHt)+π​r2​c=−g​(λ​(XHt)+H​(t,w))=−g​τλ,H.1=\hat{\lambda}(R)=a\,\lambda(X_{H_{t}})+\pi r^{2}c=-g(\lambda(X_{H_{t}})+H(t,w))=-g\,\tau_{\lambda,H}.

Hence,g=−1τλ,Hg=-\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}}, and thereforea=1τλ,H,b=∂tH2​π​r​τλ,H,c=1τλ,Ha=\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}},b=\frac{\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r\,\tau_{\lambda,H}},c=\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}}. Substituting these coefficients into the expression forRRgivesRαH=1τλ,H​(XHt+∂tH2​π​r​∂r+∂t)=1τλ,H​(XHt+∂tH2​π​r​∂r+2​π​∂θ).R_{\alpha_{H}}=\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}}\left(X_{H_{t}}+\frac{\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r}\partial_{r}+\partial_{t}\right)=\frac{1}{\tau_{\lambda,H}}\left(X_{H_{t}}+\frac{\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r}\partial_{r}+2\pi\partial_{\theta}\right).

Finally, by the defining assumptions onHH, the Hamiltonian is autonomous in a neighborhood of∂W\partial W. Hence∂tH=0\partial_{t}H=0near the locusr=0⊂∂WHr=0\subset\partial W_{H}. Therefore∂tH2​π​r​∂r\frac{\partial_{t}H}{2\pi r}\partial_{r}vanishes nearr=0r=0. The remaining term∂t=2​π​∂θ\partial_{t}=2\pi\partial_{\theta}is the angular vector field in theℂ\mathbb{C}-factor and extends smoothly acrossr=0r=0, where it vanishes. Thus the above formula defines a smooth vector field on all of∂WH\partial W_{H}.
∎

## 2.3.Floer theory

We briefly recall the Floer-theoretic setup used throughout the paper, in particular, on results in Section1.9. Let(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W})be a Liouville domain and denote byW^=W∪∂W([1,∞)r×∂W)\widehat{W}=W\cup_{\partial W}([1,\infty)_{r}\times\partial W)its Liouville completion. On the cylindrical end we writeλ^W=r​αW\widehat{\lambda}_{W}=r\alpha_{W}, whereαW=λW|∂W\alpha_{W}=\lambda_{W}|_{\partial W}.

A HamiltonianKN:S1×W^→ℝK_{N}:S^{1}\times\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{R}is called admissible if it isC2C^{2}-small on the compact partWWand, on the cylindrical end, agrees with a function of the radial coordinate which is linear of slopeN>0N>0forr≫1r\gg 1. We always chooseNNoutside the Reeb spectrum of(∂W,αW)(\partial W,\alpha_{W}). This condition ensures that no new11-periodic Hamiltonian orbits appear on the region whereKNK_{N}is exactly linear. After a small perturbation in the transition region, the set𝒫​(KN)\mathcal{P}(K_{N})of11-periodic orbits ofKNK_{N}is non-degenerate. Note that the orbits in the transition region correspond, in the usual way, to Reeb orbits on(∂W,αW)(\partial W,\alpha_{W})with period controlled by the slopeNN.

LetJNJ_{N}be a compatible almost complex structure which is cylindrical on the end. In particular, on the cylindrical endJNJ_{N}is invariant in the radial direction and is adapted to the contact formαW\alpha_{W}. We define the Floer chain complex overℚ\mathbb{Q}byCF∗⁡(KN,JN)≔ℚ​⟨𝒫​(KN)⟩\operatorname{CF}_{*}(K_{N},J_{N})\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{P}(K_{N})\rangle. The differential is defined by counting finite-energy solutionsu:ℝ×S1→W^u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to\widehat{W}of the Floer equation∂su+JN​(∂tu−XKN​(u))=0\partial_{s}u+J_{N}(\partial_{t}u-X_{K_{N}}(u))=0with asymptoticslims→−∞u​(s,⋅)=γ−,lims→+∞u​(s,⋅)=γ+\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,\cdot)=\gamma_{-},\lim_{s\to+\infty}u(s,\cdot)=\gamma_{+}, whereγ±∈𝒫​(KN)\gamma_{\pm}\in\mathcal{P}(K_{N}). The standard maximum principle for admissible Hamiltonians and cylindrical almost complex structures implies that these Floer cylinders remain in a compact subset ofW^\widehat{W}. Hence the usual compactness and gluing arguments apply, and for a generic choice ofJNJ_{N}we obtain a well-defined chain complex(CF∗⁡(KN,JN),∂KN,JN)(\operatorname{CF}_{*}(K_{N},J_{N}),\partial_{K_{N},J_{N}}). We denote its homology byHF∗⁡(KN,JN)\operatorname{HF}_{*}(K_{N},J_{N}).

IfN≤N′N\leq N^{\prime}, we choose a monotone homotopy fromKNK_{N}toKN′K_{N^{\prime}}. Counting the corresponding parametrized Floer solutions gives a continuation mapHF∗⁡(KN,JN)→HF∗⁡(KN′,JN′)\operatorname{HF}_{*}(K_{N},J_{N})\to\operatorname{HF}_{*}(K_{N^{\prime}},J_{N^{\prime}}). These maps are compatible under composition. Taking the direct limit over any cofinal sequence of admissible slopes gives the symplectic homologySH∗​(W)≔lim→N⁡HF∗​(KN,JN).{\rm SH}_{*}(W)\coloneqq\varinjlim_{N}{\rm HF}_{*}(K_{N},J_{N}).

The resulting group is independent of all auxiliary choices, including the cofinal family{KN}\{K_{N}\}, the perturbations, and the almost complex structures.

We can also apply the filtered or theS1S^{1}-equivariant versions of this construction. The action functional, with the above Hamiltonian convention, is𝒜K​(γ)=∫S1(γ∗​λ^W+Kt​(γ​(t))​d​t)\mathcal{A}_{K}(\gamma)=\int_{S^{1}}(\gamma^{*}\widehat{\lambda}_{W}+K_{t}(\gamma(t))\,dt). It induces an action filtration on the Floer complex, and the continuation maps are compatible with this filtration in the standard monotone sense. Applying the same cofinal family of admissible Hamiltonians to theS1S^{1}-equivariant Floer complex givesSH∗S1⁡(W,λW)\operatorname{SH}_{*}^{S^{1}}(W,\lambda_{W}). In the quantitative applications below, we use the filtered positiveS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology and the associated persistence module/barcode.

## 2.4.Quantitative homological tools

In this subsection, we will use the barcode ofS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology to estimate the symplectic Banach–Mazur distance. We refer[34,40]for more details. Now we begin with recalling the following definition.

Anℝ+\mathbb{R}_{+}-persistence module𝕍\mathbb{V}consists of a collection of vector spacesVsV_{s}, indexed bys∈ℝ+s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}, together with linear maps (the “structure maps”)σt,s𝕍:Vs→Vt\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{t,s}\colon V_{s}\rightarrow V_{t}fors≤ts\leq t, such thatσs,s𝕍=idVs\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{s,s}=\operatorname{id}_{V_{s}}andσu,s𝕍=σu,t𝕍∘σt,s𝕍​whenever​s≤t≤u\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{u,s}=\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{u,t}\circ\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{t,s}\text{ whenever }s\leq t\leq u.
If𝕍={Vs,σt,s𝕍}\mathbb{V}=\{V_{s},\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{t,s}\}and𝕎={Ws,σt,s𝕎}\mathbb{W}=\{W_{s},\sigma^{\mathbb{W}}_{t,s}\}areℝ+\mathbb{R}_{+}-persistence modules, amorphismF:𝕍→𝕎F\colon\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{W}is a collection of linear mapsFs:Vs→WsF_{s}\colon V_{s}\rightarrow W_{s}satisfyingFt∘σt,s𝕍=σt,s𝕎∘Fs​whenever​s≤tF_{t}\circ\sigma^{\mathbb{V}}_{t,s}=\sigma^{\mathbb{W}}_{t,s}\circ F_{s}\text{ whenever }s\leq t.

## Remark 2.11.

There is a natural correspondence betweenℝ+\mathbb{R}_{+}-persistence module andℝ\mathbb{R}-persistence module. Given anℝ+\mathbb{R}_{+}-persistence module𝕍={{Vt}t∈ℝ+,{σt,s}s≤t}\mathbb{V}=\{\{V_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}},\{\sigma_{t,s}\}_{s\leq t}\}, its logarithmic version𝕍′\mathbb{V}^{\prime}is theℝ\mathbb{R}-persistence module defined by𝕍′={{Vt′≔Vet}t∈ℝ,{σt,s′=σet,es}s≤t}.\mathbb{V}^{\prime}=\left\{\{V^{\prime}_{t}\coloneqq V_{e^{t}}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}},\{\sigma^{\prime}_{t,s}=\sigma_{e^{t},e^{s}}\}_{s\leq t}\right\}.

For any Liouville domain(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W}), one can define itsS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology. According to[19], the filtered positiveS1S^{1}-equivariant symplectic homology defines anℝ+\mathbb{R}_{+}-persistence moduleℂ​ℍ​(W,λW)={{CHL​(W,λW)}L∈ℝ+,{ιL2,L1}L2,L1∈ℝ+,L1≤L2}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{H}(W,\lambda_{W})=\left\{\{{\rm CH}^{L}(W,\lambda_{W})\}_{L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}},\{\iota_{L_{2},L_{1}}\}_{L_{2},L_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},L_{1}\leq L_{2}}\right\}

whereCHL​(W,λW){\rm CH}^{L}(W,\lambda_{W})is aℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space andιL2,L1:CHL1​(W,λW)→CHL2​(W,λW)\iota_{L_{2},L_{1}}\colon{\rm CH}^{L_{1}}(W,\lambda_{W})\rightarrow{\rm CH}^{L_{2}}(W,\lambda_{W})is aℚ\mathbb{Q}-linear map. By Remark2.11, we can obtain a logarithmic version ofS1S^{1}-equivariant persistence moduleℂ​ℍ′​(W,λW)\mathbb{C}\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(W,\lambda_{W}). Moreover, we can define the distance between theℝ\mathbb{R}-persistence modules by introducing the concept of interleaving.
Let𝕍={Vs,σt,s}\mathbb{V}=\{V_{s},\sigma_{t,s}\}and𝕎={Ws,τt,s}\mathbb{W}=\{W_{s},\tau_{t,s}\}be twoℝ\mathbb{R}-persistence modules and letδ>0\delta>0. Aδ\delta-interleavingbetween𝕍\mathbb{V}and𝕎\mathbb{W}consists of linear mapsφt:Vt→Wt+δ​and​ψt:Wt→Vt+δ\varphi_{t}\colon V_{t}\rightarrow W_{t+\delta}\text{ and }\psi_{t}\colon W_{t}\rightarrow V_{t+\delta}, for allt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R},
which commute with all structure maps: for everys≤ts\leq t,τt+δ,s+δ∘φs=φt∘σt,s,σt+δ,s+δ∘ψs=ψt∘τt,s.\tau_{t+\delta,s+\delta}\circ\varphi_{s}=\varphi_{t}\circ\sigma_{t,s},\qquad\sigma_{t+\delta,s+\delta}\circ\psi_{s}=\psi_{t}\circ\tau_{t,s}.

They are also required to satisfy, for everyt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R},ψt+δ∘φt=σt+2​δ,tandφt+δ∘ψt=τt+2​δ,t.\psi_{t+\delta}\circ\varphi_{t}=\sigma_{t+2\delta,t}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi_{t+\delta}\circ\psi_{t}=\tau_{t+2\delta,t}.

Theinterleaving distancebetween𝕍\mathbb{V}and𝕎\mathbb{W}isdint​(𝕍,𝕎)≔inf{δ≥0∣𝕍​and​𝕎​admit aδ-interleaving}.d_{\rm int}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})\coloneqq\inf\{\delta\geq 0\mid\mathbb{V}\text{ and }\mathbb{W}\text{ admit a $\delta$-interleaving}\}.

By the Normal Form Theorem, when the persistence module𝕍\mathbb{V}is pointwise finite dimensional, we can decompose it into a direct sum of “interval type” persistence modules of the formℚIi\mathbb{Q}_{I_{i}}supported only in the intervalIi⊂ℝI_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}with pointwise dimension 1. We collect these intervalsIiI_{i}with multiplicitiesmim_{i}asℬ​(𝕍)\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{V}), thebarcodeof𝕍\mathbb{V}.

We next define a distance between barcodes. For an intervalII, letIδI^{\delta}denote the interval obtained by expandingIIoutward byδ\deltaat both finite endpoints. For a barcodeℬ\mathcal{B}andε>0\varepsilon>0, letℬε\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}be the sub-multiset of bars inℬ\mathcal{B}whose lengths are greater thanε\varepsilon. Amatchingbetween two barcodesℬ\mathcal{B}and𝒞\mathcal{C}is a bijectionμ:ℬ′→𝒞′\mu\colon\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}between sub-multisetsℬ′⊂ℬ\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}and𝒞′⊂𝒞\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}. Thusdom⁡μ=ℬ′\operatorname{dom}\mu=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}andim⁡μ=𝒞′\operatorname{im}\mu=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}; bars outside these sub-multisets are left unmatched.

Aδ\delta-matchingbetweenℬ\mathcal{B}and𝒞\mathcal{C}is such a partial matchingμ:ℬ′→𝒞′\mu\colon\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}satisfying
- (1)

ℬ2​δ⊂dom​μ.\mathcal{B}_{2\delta}\subset{\rm dom}\mu.,
- (2)

𝒞2​δ⊂Im⁡μ\mathcal{C}_{2\delta}\subset\operatorname{Im}\mu,
- (3)

Ifμ​(I)=J\mu(I)=J, thenI⊂JδI\subset J^{\delta},J⊂IδJ\subset I^{\delta}.

Thebottleneck distancebetweenℬ\mathcal{B}and𝒞\mathcal{C}isdbot​(ℬ,𝒞):=inf{δ>0∣ℬ​and​𝒞​admit aδ-matching}.d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}):=\inf\{\delta>0\mid\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\mathcal{C}\text{ admit a $\delta$-matching}\}.

## Theorem 2.12(Isometry Theorem).

For any two persistence modules𝕍\mathbb{V}and𝕎\mathbb{W}, we havedint​(𝕍,𝕎)=dbot​(ℬ​(𝕍),ℬ​(𝕎)).d_{\rm int}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})=d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{V}),\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{W})).

Denote the barcode ofℂ​ℍ′​(W,λW)\mathbb{C}\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(W,\lambda_{W})andℂ​ℍ′​(V,λV)\mathbb{C}\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(V,\lambda_{V})byℬ​(W,λW)\mathcal{B}(W,\lambda_{W})andℬ​(V,λV)\mathcal{B}(V,\lambda_{V}). Then we can use the bottleneck distance to estimate the symplectic Banach–Mazur distance.

## Proposition 2.13(Theorem 1.8 in[38]or Proposition 3.1 in[40]).

For any two Liouville domains(W,λW)(W,\lambda_{W})and(V,λV)(V,\lambda_{V}), we havedbot​(ℬ​(W,λW),ℬ​(V,λV))≤dSBM​((W,λW),(V,λV)).d_{\rm bot}(\mathcal{B}(W,\lambda_{W}),\mathcal{B}(V,\lambda_{V}))\leq d_{\rm SBM}((W,\lambda_{W}),(V,\lambda_{V})).

## 2.5.Equicontinuity and entropy

## Proposition 2.14.

Letφt:X→X\varphi^{t}\colon X\rightarrow Xbe an equicontinuous flow. Thenhtop​(φ)=0h_{\rm top}(\varphi)=0.

## Proof.

Letggbe any Riemannian metric onXXand denote byddthe induced Riemannian distance, which generates the topology ofXX. We define a newφ\varphi-invariant metric onXXbyd′​(x,y)≔supt∈ℝd​(φt​(x),φt​(y)),for any​x,y∈X.d^{\prime}(x,y)\coloneqq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}d(\varphi^{t}(x),\varphi^{t}(y)),\text{ for any }x,y\in X.

Equicontinuity ofφt\varphi^{t}guarantees thatd′d^{\prime}is finite and induces the same topology asdd. Moreover,d′d^{\prime}is invariant under the flow:d′​(φt​(x),φt​(y))=d′​(x,y)d^{\prime}(\varphi^{t}(x),\varphi^{t}(y))=d^{\prime}(x,y)for alltt. Because of this invariance, two pointsx,yx,yare(T,ε)(T,\varepsilon)-separated with respect tod′d^{\prime}if and only ifd′​(x,y)≥εd^{\prime}(x,y)\geq\varepsilon. Consequently the maximal cardinalityNsep​(φ,T,ε,d′)N_{\mathrm{sep}}(\varphi,T,\varepsilon,d^{\prime})equals the maximal cardinality of anε\varepsilon-separated set in the compact metric space(X,d′)(X,d^{\prime}). This number is finite and independent ofTT. Hencelim supT→∞1T​ln⁡Nsep​(φ,T,ε,d′)=0\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\ln N_{\mathrm{sep}}(\varphi,T,\varepsilon,d^{\prime})=0for everyε>0\varepsilon>0, and thereforehtop​(φ)=0h_{\mathrm{top}}(\varphi)=0.
∎

## 2.6.Estimation of capacity

The proof of Proposition1.25relies on a technique from symplectically uniruled manifolds. Recall a closed symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega)is calledsymplectically uniruledif there exists a nonzero genus-zero Gromov–Witten invariant of the form⟨pt,a2,…,ak⟩k,AM,\langle\mathrm{pt},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\rangle_{k,A}^{M},wherek≥1k\geq 1,0≠A∈H2​(M;ℤ)0\neq A\in H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}), andai∈H∗​(M;ℚ)a_{i}\in H^{*}(M;\mathbb{Q}). The following proposition is essentially due to[28].

## Proposition 2.15.

If(M,ω)(M,\omega)is symplectically uniruled, thenwG​(M×ℂ)<∞w_{G}(M\times\mathbb{C})<\infty. In particular, if(W,ω)(W,\omega)can be symplectically embedded into a closed symplectically uniruled manifold, thenwG​(W×ℂ)<∞w_{G}(W\times\mathbb{C})<\infty.

## Proof.

By assumption, there exist a homology classA≠0A\neq 0and classesa2,…,aka_{2},\dots,a_{k}such that⟨pt,a2,…,ak⟩k,AM≠0.\langle\mathrm{pt},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\rangle_{k,A}^{M}\neq 0.Since this invariant is nonzero andA≠0A\neq 0, we haveω​(A)>0\omega(A)>0, soAAis non-torsion. Hence there is a classd∈H2​(M;ℚ)d\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Q})such that⟨d,A⟩≠0\langle d,A\rangle\neq 0. Setr≔max⁡{0,3−k}r\coloneqq\max\{0,3-k\}andm≔k+rm\coloneqq k+r, and letb2,…,bmb_{2},\dots,b_{m}be the lista2,…,aka_{2},\dots,a_{k}followed byrrcopies ofdd. The divisor axiom gives⟨pt,b2,…,bm⟩m,AM=⟨d,A⟩r​⟨pt,a2,…,ak⟩k,AM≠0\langle\mathrm{pt},b_{2},\dots,b_{m}\rangle_{m,A}^{M}=\langle d,A\rangle^{r}\langle\mathrm{pt},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\rangle_{k,A}^{M}\neq 0. In particular,m≥3m\geq 3.

Fora>0a>0, letS2​(a)=(S2,ωa)S^{2}(a)=(S^{2},\omega_{a})denote the symplectic two-sphere of total area∫S2ωa=a\int_{S^{2}}\omega_{a}=a. Then we considerXa≔M×S2​(a),Ωa≔ω⊕ωa,and​B≔(A,0)∈H2​(Xa;ℤ).X_{a}\coloneqq M\times S^{2}(a),\,\,\Omega_{a}\coloneqq\omega\oplus\omega_{a},\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,B\coloneqq(A,0)\in H_{2}(X_{a};\mathbb{Z}).

Fori=2,…,mi=2,\dots,m, defineb~i≔pr1∗​bi∈H∗​(Xa;ℚ)\widetilde{b}_{i}\coloneqq{\rm pr}_{1}^{*}b_{i}\in H^{*}(X_{a};\mathbb{Q}). WritingptXa=pr1∗​(ptM)∪pr2∗​(ptS2​(a))\mathrm{pt}_{X_{a}}={\rm pr}_{1}^{*}(\mathrm{pt}_{M})\cup{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}(\mathrm{pt}_{S^{2}(a)}), observe that every genus-zero stable map toXaX_{a}in classB=(A,0)B=(A,0)is constant in theS2​(a)S^{2}(a)-factor. Thus the corresponding virtual moduli cycle is the product of the virtual moduli cycle for maps toMMin classAAwith[S2​(a)][S^{2}(a)]. Pairing the latter factor withptS2​(a)\mathrm{pt}_{S^{2}(a)}gives, equivalently by the genus-zero product formula,⟨ptXa,b~2,…,b~m⟩m,BXa=⟨pt,b2,…,bm⟩m,AM≠0.\langle\mathrm{pt}_{X_{a}},\widetilde{b}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{b}_{m}\rangle^{X_{a}}_{m,B}=\langle\mathrm{pt},b_{2},\dots,b_{m}\rangle^{M}_{m,A}\neq 0.

SinceB≠0B\neq 0andm≥3m\geq 3, Theorem 4.1 of[23]applies and yieldswG​(Xa)≤Ωa​(B)=ω​(A).w_{G}(X_{a})\leq\Omega_{a}(B)=\omega(A).

Letc>0c>0and suppose that there exists a symplectic embeddingφ:B2​n+2​(c)↪M×ℂ\varphi\colon B^{2n+2}(c)\hookrightarrow M\times\mathbb{C}. For any0<c′<c0<c^{\prime}<c, the setφ​(B¯2​n+2​(c′))\varphi(\overline{B}^{\,2n+2}(c^{\prime}))is compact. Hence its projection to theℂ\mathbb{C}-factor is contained inB2​(a)B^{2}(a)for somea>0a>0. Therefore the restriction ofφ\varphigivesB2​n+2​(c′)↪M×B2​(a)↪M×S2​(a)B^{2n+2}(c^{\prime})\hookrightarrow M\times B^{2}(a)\hookrightarrow M\times S^{2}(a). It follows thatc′≤wG​(M×S2​(a))≤ω​(A)c^{\prime}\leq w_{G}(M\times S^{2}(a))\leq\omega(A). Lettingc′→cc^{\prime}\rightarrow c, we obtainc≤ω​(A)c\leq\omega(A). Taking the supremum over all suchccproves thatwG​(M×ℂ)≤ω​(A)<∞w_{G}(M\times\mathbb{C})\leq\omega(A)<\infty.

## Example 2.16.

Every HamiltonianS1S^{1}-manifold is symplectically uniruled by[31].

The following result is another elementary result that will be useful to the proof of Proposition1.25.

## Lemma 2.17.

Let(Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega)be a connected compact symplectic surface, possibly with boundary. ThenwG​(Σ,ω)=∫Σω.w_{G}(\Sigma,\omega)=\int_{\Sigma}\omega.

## Proof.

Suppose that there exists a symplectic embeddingφ:B2​(r)↪Σ\varphi\colon B^{2}(r)\hookrightarrow\Sigma. Then we haver=∫B2​(r)ωstd=∫φ​(B2​(r))ω≤∫Σωr=\int_{B^{2}(r)}\omega_{\mathrm{std}}=\int_{\varphi(B^{2}(r))}\omega\leq\int_{\Sigma}\omega, hencewG​(Σ,ω)≤∫Σω.w_{G}(\Sigma,\omega)\leq\int_{\Sigma}\omega.

For the reverse inequality, fixε>0\varepsilon>0. By the classification of compact connected surfaces,Σ\Sigmacan be represented as a quotient of a planar polygonPPby pairwise edge identifications, possibly after removing finitely many disjoint open disks to account for the boundary. Letq:P→Σq\colon P\to\Sigmabe the quotient map. Thenqqis injective on the interior ofPP.

Choose a smoothly bounded domainDεD_{\varepsilon}, compactly supported inInt​(P){\rm Int}(P)and diffeomorphic to a disk, such that∫Dεq∗​ω>∫Σω−ε\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}q^{*}\omega>\int_{\Sigma}\omega-\varepsilon. By Moser’s theorem in dimension two, the symplectic disk(Dε,q∗​ω)(D_{\varepsilon},q^{*}\omega)is symplectomorphic to the standard diskB¯2​(rε)\overline{B}^{\,2}(r_{\varepsilon})withrε=∫Dεq∗​ωr_{\varepsilon}=\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}q^{*}\omega. Sinceqqis injective onDεD_{\varepsilon}, this gives a symplectic embeddingB¯2​(rε)↪Σ\overline{B}^{\,2}(r_{\varepsilon})\hookrightarrow\Sigma, and thereforewG​(Σ,ω)≥rε>∫Σω−εw_{G}(\Sigma,\omega)\geq r_{\varepsilon}>\int_{\Sigma}\omega-\varepsilon. Lettingε→0\varepsilon\to 0proves the lemma.
∎

## 3.Proof of TheoremA

Before proving TheoremA, we first show some basic properties of the Thompson-type distancedTd_{\rm T}onℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda).

## Proposition 3.1.
- (1)

We have the following equivalent expression,dT​(H,G)=infφ∈Symp​(W,λ)supS1×Int​(W)|ln⁡H−ln⁡(G∘φ)|=infφ∈Symp​(W,λ)‖ln⁡H−ln⁡(G∘φ)‖∞.d_{\rm T}(H,G)=\inf_{\varphi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)}\sup_{S^{1}\times{\rm Int}(W)}\bigl|\ln H-\ln(G\circ\varphi)\bigr|=\inf_{\varphi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)}\|\ln H-\ln(G\circ\varphi)\|_{\infty}.
- (2)

The Thompson-type distancedTd_{\rm T}satisfies the basic metric properties:
- •

dT​(H,G)=dT​(G,H)d_{\rm T}(H,G)=d_{\rm T}(G,H).
- •

dT​(H,G)≥0d_{\rm T}(H,G)\geq 0.
- •

For anyH,G,F∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G,F\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda),dT​(H,F)≤dT​(H,G)+dT​(G,F)d_{\rm T}(H,F)\leq d_{\rm T}(H,G)+d_{\rm T}(G,F).

Consequently,dTd_{\rm T}is an extended pseudo-metric onℋ\mathcal{H}(i.e., it may take the value+∞+\infty).
- (3)

The Thompson-type distancedTd_{\rm T}satisfies the following invariance properties:
- •

For anyφ,ψ∈Symp​(W,λ)\varphi,\psi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda)andH,G∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), we havedT​(H,G)=dT​(H∘φ,G∘ψ).d_{\rm T}(H,G)=d_{\rm T}(H\circ\varphi,G\circ\psi).
- •

For any constanta>0a>0,dT​(a​H,a​G)=dT​(H,G)d_{\rm T}(aH,aG)=d_{\rm T}(H,G).
- •

DefineHt0​(t,x)≔H​(t+t0,x)H_{t_{0}}(t,x)\coloneqq H(t+t_{0},x)for anyt0∈S1t_{0}\in S^{1}. ThendT​(Ht0,Gt0)=dT​(H,G)d_{\rm T}(H_{t_{0}},G_{t_{0}})=d_{\rm T}(H,G).

## Proof.

The verification of (1) and (3) is straightforward and therefore omitted.

For (2), the first bullet comes from the following equivalence,1C​H≤G∘φ≤C​H⟺1C​G≤H∘φ−1≤C​G.\frac{1}{C}H\leq G\circ\varphi\leq CH\Longleftrightarrow\frac{1}{C}G\leq H\circ\varphi^{-1}\leq CG.

The second bullet is trivial. For the third bullet, if for someC,D≥1C,D\geq 1andφ,ψ∈Symp​(W,λ)\varphi,\psi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda), we have1C​H≤G∘φ≤C​H,1D​G≤F∘ψ≤D​G\frac{1}{C}H\leq G\circ\varphi\leq CH,\frac{1}{D}G\leq F\circ\psi\leq DG

then1C​D​H≤1D​G∘φ≤F∘ψ∘φ≤D​G∘φ≤C​D​H.∎\frac{1}{CD}H\leq\frac{1}{D}G\circ\varphi\leq F\circ\psi\circ\varphi\leq D\,G\circ\varphi\leq CD\,H.\qed

For the next proposition, recall thatℋ+​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda)is a subset ofℋ​(W,λ)\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda)with an extra condition thatλ​(XHt)≥0\lambda(X_{H_{t}})\geq 0.

## Proof of TheoremA.

By Proposition3.2, for anyH,G∈ℋ+​(W,λ)H,G\in\mathcal{H}^{+}(W,\lambda), we havedSBM​(U​(H),U​(G))≤inf{ln⁡C≥0|1C​H≤G≤C​H}.d_{\rm SBM}({\rm U}(H),{\rm U}(G))\leq\inf\left\{\ln C\geq 0\,\left|\,\frac{1}{C}H\leq G\leq CH\right\}\right..

Then we havedSBM​(U​(H),U​(G))\displaystyle d_{\rm SBM}({\rm U}(H),{\rm U}(G))≤inf{ln⁡C|∃φ∈Symp​(W,λ),s.t.​1C​H≤G∘φ≤C​H}\displaystyle\leq\inf\left\{\ln C\,\left|\,\exists\,\varphi\in{\rm Symp}(W,\lambda),\text{ s.t. }\frac{1}{C}H\leq G\circ\varphi\leq CH\right\}\right.=dT​(H,G).\displaystyle=d_{\rm T}(H,G).

This completes the proof. ∎

## Proposition 4.2.

The distancedCBMd_{\rm CBM}satisfies the following properties. For anyH,G,K∈ℋ​(W,λ)H,G,K\in\mathcal{H}(W,\lambda), anyφ∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH)\varphi\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H})andψ∈Cont0​(∂WG,ker⁡αG)\psi\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{G},\ker\alpha_{G}),

dCBM​(αH,αG)≥0d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{G})\geq 0anddCBM​(αH,αH)=0d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{H})=0;

For (2), for anyφ,φ0∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH)\varphi,\varphi_{0}\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}), ifφ0∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef​φ∗​αH=φ∗​(ef∘φ−1​αH),\varphi^{*}_{0}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}(\alpha_{G})=e^{f}\varphi^{*}\alpha_{H}=\varphi^{*}(e^{f\circ\varphi^{-1}}\alpha_{H}),then(φ−1)∗​φ0∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef∘φ−1​αH.(\varphi^{-1})^{*}\varphi^{*}_{0}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}(\alpha_{G})=e^{f\circ\varphi^{-1}}\alpha_{H}.By definition,dCBM​(φ∗​αH,αG)\displaystyle d_{\rm CBM}(\varphi^{*}\alpha_{H},\alpha_{G})=inf{lnC|∃φ0∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH),s.t.φ0∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef​φ∗​αH,|f|≤ln⁡C}\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\ln C\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\varphi_{0}\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),\text{ s.t.}\\
\varphi^{*}_{0}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}(\alpha_{G})=e^{f}\varphi^{*}\alpha_{H},|f|\leq\ln C\end{array}\right\}\right.=inf{lnC|∃φ1=φ0∘φ∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH),s.t.φ1∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef∘φ−1​αH,|f∘φ−1|≤ln⁡C}\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\ln C\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),\text{ s.t.}\\

For anyψ∈Cont0​(∂WG,ker⁡αG)\psi\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{G},\ker\alpha_{G}), sinceφG,H∘φH,G=Id∂WH\varphi_{G,H}\circ\varphi_{H,G}=\operatorname{Id}_{\partial W_{H}}, we can defineφ≔φG,H∘ψ∘φH,G∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH).\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{G,H}\circ\psi\circ\varphi_{H,G}\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}).Equivalently,ψ=φH,G∘φ∘φG,H\psi=\varphi_{H,G}\circ\varphi\circ\varphi_{G,H}, and the pullbacks satisfyφ∗​φH,G∗=φH,G∗​ψ∗.\varphi^{*}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}=\varphi_{H,G}^{*}\psi^{*}.This gives an identification ofCont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH){\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H})andCont0​(∂WG,ker⁡αG){\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{G},\ker\alpha_{G}).
By definition,dCBM​(αH,ψ∗​αG)\displaystyle d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\psi^{*}\alpha_{G})=inf{lnC|∃φ0∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH),s.t.φ0∗​φH,G∗​ψ∗​(αG)=ef​αH,|f|≤ln⁡C}\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\ln C\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\varphi_{0}\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),\text{ s.t.}\\
\varphi^{*}_{0}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}\psi^{*}(\alpha_{G})=e^{f}\alpha_{H},|f|\leq\ln C\end{array}\right\}\right.=inf{lnC|∃φ1=φ∘φ0∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH),s.t.φ1∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef​αH,|f|≤ln⁡C}\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\ln C\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\varphi_{1}=\varphi\circ\varphi_{0}\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),\text{ s.t.}\\
\varphi^{*}_{1}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}(\alpha_{G})=e^{f}\alpha_{H},|f|\leq\ln C\end{array}\right\}\right.=dCBM​(αH,αG).\displaystyle=d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{G}).

For (3), by definition,dCBM​(αH,αG)\displaystyle d_{\rm CBM}\left(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{G}\right)=inf{lnC|∃φ∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH),s.t.φ∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef​αH,|f|≤ln⁡C}\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\ln C\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\varphi\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),\text{ s.t.}\\

The second equality uses the conjugation identityφ∗​φH,G∗=φH,G∗​ψ∗\varphi^{*}\varphi_{H,G}^{*}=\varphi_{H,G}^{*}\psi^{*}above. The third and fourth equalities follow by pulling back withφG,H\varphi_{G,H}andψ−1\psi^{-1}, respectively.

For (4), by definition,dCBM​(φH,G∗​(αG),φH,K∗​(αK))=\displaystyle d_{\rm CBM}\left(\varphi_{H,G}^{*}(\alpha_{G}),\varphi_{H,K}^{*}(\alpha_{K})\right)=inf{lnC|∃φ∈Cont0​(∂WH,ker⁡αH),s.t.φ∗​φH,G∗​(αG)=ef​φH,K∗​(αK),|f|≤ln⁡C}\displaystyle\inf\left\{\ln C\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\varphi\in{\rm Cont}_{0}(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),\text{ s.t.}\\

The second equality uses the same conjugation identity. For the third equality, pull back byφG,H\varphi_{G,H}and useφG,K=φH,K∘φG,H\varphi_{G,K}=\varphi_{H,K}\circ\varphi_{G,H}.

For (5), by (3) in Lemma 7 in[36], the triangle inequality ofdCBMd_{\rm CBM}on(∂WH,ker⁡αH)(\partial W_{H},\ker\alpha_{H}),dCBM​(αH,αK)\displaystyle d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{K})=dCBM​(αH,φH,K∗​αK)\displaystyle=d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\varphi_{H,K}^{*}\alpha_{K})≤dCBM​(αH,φH,G∗​αG)+dCBM​(φH,G∗​αG,φH,K∗​αK)\displaystyle\leq d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\varphi_{H,G}^{*}\alpha_{G})+d_{\rm CBM}(\varphi^{*}_{H,G}\alpha_{G},\varphi^{*}_{H,K}\alpha_{K})=dCBM​(αH,αG)+dCBM​(αG,αK).∎\displaystyle=d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{H},\alpha_{G})+d_{\rm CBM}(\alpha_{G},\alpha_{K}).\qed

Now, we can give the proof of TheoremB
